<meta>This page serves as an introduction to a digital archival copy, explaining its origin and the rules for its use. While the source was labeled as Greek, the provided text is the standard English disclaimer for digitized public domain works.</meta>

This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project to make the world’s books discoverable online.

It has survived long enough for the **copyright** <term>copyright<note>A legal right that grants the creator of an original work exclusive rights to its use and distribution.</note></term> to expire and the book to enter the **public domain** <term>public domain<note>Works whose intellectual property rights have expired, meaning they belong to the public and can be used by anyone without permission.</note></term>. A public domain book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary country to country. Public domain books are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture and knowledge that’s often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations and other **marginalia** <term>marginalia<note>Handwritten notes, sketches, or marks made in the margins of a book by previous owners or librarians.</note></term> present in the original volume will appear in this file - a reminder of this book’s long journey from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

## Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying.

We also ask that you:

+ *Make non-commercial use of the files* We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.

+ *Refrain from automated querying* Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google’s system: If you are conducting research on machine translation, **optical character recognition** <term>optical character recognition (OCR)<note>Technology used to convert images of text into machine-readable and searchable text.</note></term> or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.

+ *Maintain attribution* The Google “watermark” you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.

+ *Keep it legal* Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we can’t offer guidance on whether any specific use of any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book’s appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liability can be quite severe.

## About Google Book Search

Google’s mission is to organize the world’s information and to make it universally accessible and useful. Google Book Search helps readers discover the world’s books while helping authors and publishers reach new audiences. --- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory front matter of the volume, following a blank leaf. It serves as the editor's preface, detailing his motivations, travels, and the specific biblical manuscripts contained in this collection.</meta>

<page-num>XI</page-num>
<sig>b*</sig>

Since, by God's providence, it has happened that the records of His book—than which nothing more sacred or greater has been given to the human race—written during the first nine centuries of the Christian era, were not destroyed in the same ruin that claimed nearly all other books of similar antiquity, and since their authority in the whole Church of Christ is and always remains supreme, it seemed a great service to Christian scholarship to publish such monuments with the utmost care. 

While in the previous age, especially in England, this had been done here and there with several very famous biblical manuscripts (<term>codices</term> <note>original: "codicibus"; ancient manuscript books</note>) being printed, sixteen years ago I personally undertook the plan to search throughout the world for whatever remained of such antiquity and dignity, and from that to create a **Critical Library of Sacred Monuments** (<term>Bibliotheca Monumentorum Sacrorum Critica</term>). In this library, the oldest books would be represented as accurately as possible and placed, as if in a treasury, within the corpus of Christian literature. This undertaking, involving six years of travel—five through Europe and two through the East—succeeded so well that I have already published nine volumes containing twenty-two Greek or Latin manuscripts of the New and Old Testaments, which I either discovered for the first time or was certainly the first to examine thoroughly. 

The first volume of this "New Collection" includes six of the most important books, for the most part discovered by my own efforts in the East. To complete this undertaking, I have decided to add four more volumes to the first. As for the second volume of this same collection which we have just now released, I am happy to report that it again contains remains of the sacred text, a wealth of material for which I congratulate both myself and Christian scholarship. Of these, six are parts of manuscripts written between the fourth and sixth centuries and are to be numbered among the rarest monuments of the highest Christian antiquity. 

Of the three that remain, even the most recent of them—written around the eighth century—are highly distinguished for another reason: they contain the greater part of the book of **Genesis**, which is missing from the world-famous <term>Vatican Codex</term> <note>The Codex Vaticanus, one of the oldest and most important surviving Greek Bibles, which is missing its beginning</note>, and its text is notable for showing signs of Egyptian origin more clearly than most surviving witnesses. Furthermore, four of the nine manuscripts in this volume are <term>palimpsests</term> <note>parchment from which the original writing has been erased so it can be reused for new text</note>; three of these consist of a few fragments of the Evangelist **John**, the prophet **Ezekiel**, and the third book of **Kings**; one, however, contains many parts of the Gospel of **Luke**.

Let us briefly say how it came about that we were able to publish all these. The manuscript containing Genesis written around the eighth century, which I just mentioned more specifically, is among those books I found in the East in the year 1853 and brought to Europe. Before [depositing] it in the most renowned **Bodleian Library** <note>The primary research library of the University of Oxford</note>...

<meta>Digitized by Google watermark at bottom of page</meta>

<summary>In this preface, the editor describes his mission to preserve and publish the oldest surviving biblical manuscripts from the first nine centuries. He details his extensive travels to recover these texts and highlights the inclusion of a significant 8th-century manuscript of Genesis and several rare palimpsests.</summary>

<keywords>Biblical criticism, codices, palimpsests, Genesis, Vatican Codex, Bodleian Library, manuscript discovery, New Testament, Old Testament</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues Tischendorf’s preface, moving from his general mission to the specific contents of the current volume. He details the origins of several manuscript fragments, including those from his Eastern travels and those found in the British Museum, while acknowledging his patrons and collaborators.</meta>

<page-num>XII</page-num>

I yielded to the curators and arranged for a transcription to be made in Leipzig <note>original: "Lipsiae"</note> for the sake of preparing an edition. From the same collection of books discovered by me in the East, there are three other smaller fragments: one of the Psalms, another of Genesis, and a third of the Gospel of Matthew. I rescued the first and third from the bindings of old written books; the second—a part of the same body of work whose most precious members I designated nine years ago by the name **Codex Friderico-Augustanus**—was found by me in the midst of discarded leaves. The remaining items contained in this volume, however, were all drawn by me from the treasures of the British Museum. The first place is held by a <term>palimpsest</term> <note>original: "liber palimpsestus"; a manuscript page that has been scraped clean so it can be reused for new writing</note> book, brought from Egypt to London nine years ago, from which I carefully extracted the very rich fragments of the Gospel of Luke that lay hidden beneath the Syriac text. Following this is the very famous **Codex Cottonianus**, which, having been most miserably damaged by flames one hundred and twenty-five years ago, generally presents a difficulty in reading no less than—and in some places even greater than—that found in palimpsest manuscripts. Regarding this precious treasury of letters, after there had long been an opinion that it could no longer be used for critical study, and since no one in England had stood forth in this century to attempt to restore it, I am exceedingly glad that it has now been brought entirely to light by my own studies, so far as was possible. Included as well are three smaller palimpsest fragments found in the same collection of Syriac books from which the aforementioned palimpsest of Luke was recovered. That I was permitted to transcribe these treasures of the British Museum with all care and to be the first to deliver these transcriptions to the literary world, I cannot but proclaim with a grateful heart toward the distinguished men to whom the care of preserving the manuscripts of the British Museum has been entrusted. No less grateful do I declare myself to William Cureton, whom the Theological faculty of Halle recently decorated with the highest honors for his outstanding service to the Church. For after he was the first to realize that the Greek text of Luke lay hidden under the Syriac of Severus—a matter we shall discuss more accurately later—he not only zealously wrote back to me concerning several passages about whose reading I was uncertain during editing, but also saw to it that drawings <note>original: "delineationes"</note> of the London manuscripts were sent to me, executed by the skillful hand of his own wife, after I had long waited in vain for them from the person to whom I had originally assigned the task.

Just as it is right to pay the greatest thanks to those who assisted the one preparing the work, so too do I testify to my most grateful spirit toward the patrons of the completed work. Among their number, I consider it truly glorious to have the most splendid names of so many princes along with so many directors of renowned libraries, the highest administrators of public affairs and men famous for their learning and liberality, high-ranking officials of the Roman Pontiff as well as the illustrious bishops of the Church of England, and almost all of Germany with so many and such great foreign names. This surely proves that the rewards of rigorous study and untiring labor are not sought in vain, nor can they be overwhelmed by the malice or the arts of detractors.

As for which manuscripts and which fragments of the sacred text this volume contains, as well as the location of each, we shall immediately indicate in a brief overview.

(I.) In the first place, presented on pages 1–92, are the **PALIMPSEST FRAGMENTS OF THE GOSPEL OF LUKE**. These fragments, following the chapter lists from 2 to 39 and 64 to 83, are: I, 1–13. I, 69—II, 4. II, 16–27. IV, 38—V, 5. V, 25—VI, 8. VI, 18–31. VI, 49—VII, 22. VIII, 5–15. VIII, 25—IX, 1. IX, 12–43. X, 3–16. XI, 4–27. XII, 4–15. XII, 40–52. XIII, 26—XIV, 1. XIV, 12—XV, 1.

<summary>Tischendorf describes his recovery of biblical fragments from old book bindings and discarded leaves, as well as his work on damaged and overwritten manuscripts (palimpsests) held in the British Museum. He offers high praise for his collaborator William Cureton and lists his prestigious international patrons while defending his scholarly reputation.</summary>

<keywords>Constantin von Tischendorf, Codex Friderico-Augustanus, Codex Cottonianus, British Museum, William Cureton, Gospel of Luke, palimpsest, biblical criticism, manuscript recovery</keywords>

<page-num>XIII</page-num>

15:13—16:16. 17:21—18:10. 18:22—20:20. 20:33—47. 21:12—22:6. 22:8—14. 22:42—56. 22:71—23:11. 23:38—50. <note>These citations refer to the Gospel of Luke discussed on the previous page.</note> They should be assigned to the fifth or sixth century.

(II.) In the second place, positioned on pages 93—176, are the **REMAINS RESCUED FROM THE FIRE OF THE VERY FAMOUS COTTON CODEX.** <note>The Codex Cottonianus is a 4th- or 5th-century Greek manuscript of Genesis. It was nearly destroyed in a fire at Ashburnham House in 1731.</note> These passages survive: Genesis 1:14, 16—19, 26—28; 3:1—5; 4:1, 2, 4—5, 18, 25—26; 5:1—3, 6—8, 9—10, 18—24; 6:11—15; 7:1—5, 11—16; 8:1—6, 8—9; 9:15—23; 11:4—7, 12—13, 15—17, 29—32; 12:1—8, 10, 11; 13:12—18; 14:1—3, 17—20, 22—24; 15:13—16:5; 16:16—17:10; 18:3—13, 24—30, 32, 33; 19:1—4, 12—26, 30—33, [36,] 37, 38; 20:1—4; 21:4—19 (on 17 and 19, see also page 174), 23—26; 22:2—18, 22—23:10, 13—15, 17—20; 24:1—7 (on 21 and 22, see page 174), 32—39, 52—67; 25:1—9, 11—13, 20, 21, 27—26:9, 18—20; 27:25—34, 37, 38; 28:4—6, 8—14, 16—22; 29:18—20, 25—29; 30:16—18 (on 19 and 20, see page 174); 31:26—35; 32:16—21; 33:13—15, 17 (on 19 and 20, see page 174); 34:4—11, 14—23 (on 26, 28, 29, see page 174), 30—35:14 (on 14 and 15, see page 174); 36:2—30; 37:14—30 (on 3—7, see page 174); 38:6—12, 20—21, 26—30; 39:1—40:15, 21—41:41, 46—49, 56, 57; 42:1—23, 35, 36—43:4, 6—10, 31—33; 44:5—10, 12—16, 31—45:1, 5—9, 17—21; 47:4—7; 48:15—17; 49:1, 9, 13—18, 24—27. These fragments seem to have been written in the fifth century.

(III.) In the third place, on pages 177—308, follows the **GENESIS CODEX OF THE BODLEIAN LIBRARY RECENTLY** (by Tischendorf) **BROUGHT FROM THE EAST.** It contains Genesis 1:1—14:6 (page 216); 18:24—20:14 (page 224); 24:54—42:18. It seems to have been written in the eighth century, specifically at its end.

(IV.) In the fourth place, on pages 311—312, are read the **PALIMPSEST FRAGMENTS OF THE GOSPEL OF JOHN:** 13:16—17, 19—20, 23—24, 26—27; 16:7—8, 12—13, 15—16, 18—19. They belong to the British Museum, written in the fifth or fourth century.

(V.) In the fifth place, on pages 313—314, are read the **PALIMPSEST FRAGMENTS OF THE PROPHET EZEKIEL:** 4:16—5:1, 2—4. They belong to the British Museum, written around the seventh century.

(VI.) In the sixth place, on pages 315—316, are given the **PALIMPSEST FRAGMENTS OF THE THIRD BOOK OF KINGS:** <note>In the Latin Vulgate and Greek Septuagint, the "Third Book of Kings" corresponds to what is now known in English Bibles as 1 Kings.</note> 8:58—9:1. These also belong to the British Museum and seem to have been written in the fifth century.

(VII.) In the seventh place, on pages 319—320, **TISCHENDORF’S PSALM FRAGMENTS** have been transcribed: 131(132):7—8, 132(133):1—3, 134(135):7—13. <note>The dual numbering reflects the difference between the Greek/Latin and Hebrew verse numbering.</note> They are from the fifth or fourth century.

(VIII.) In the eighth place, on page 321, is held **TISCHENDORF’S FRAGMENT OF THE BOOK OF GENESIS:** 24:9—10, 41—43. It is a part of the same book as the Codex Friderico-Augustanus, dating to around the fourth century.

(IX.) In the ninth place, on the same page, are added **TWO TISCHENDORF FRAGMENTS OF THE GOSPEL OF MATTHEW:** 12:17—19, 23—25. They are remains of the same codex from roughly the seventh century, of which some leaves were published under the name Codex Tischendorfianus I.

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical and historical introduction to the manuscript collection. On the previous page, Tischendorf listed the biblical contents of the Cotton Genesis fragments; here, he concludes his remarks on the typography used in this edition and begins the detailed history of the "palimpsest treasure" discovered in the Nitrian desert.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>XIV</page-num>

All of these matters must now be discussed more fully and individually. Before I do so, I will speak briefly about the typefaces <note>original: "typis"</note> that have been used in this volume, as in the previous one, to represent the texts of the ancient manuscripts. In the preface to the first volume, I stated that these were prepared by the highly skilled printers Giesecke and Devrient, under my own guidance and authority, so that the **uncial letters** <note>Uncial is a script written entirely in large, rounded capital letters, characteristic of Greek and Latin manuscripts from the 4th to 8th centuries.</note> found in the oldest Greek parchments might be faithfully reproduced. Just as we trusted that this would be most welcome to learned men, it has indeed been greatly approved by many.*)

However, since these same typefaces were also used by us to print those manuscripts which have already deviated in various ways from the most ancient style of uncial writing—such as the Uffenbachian manuscript of the Pauline Epistles <note>original: "codex epistularum Paulinarum Uffenbachianus"</note> published in my *Anecdota*, and the Oxford Genesis manuscript included in this volume—I explained in the preface to my *Sacred and Profane Anecdotes* (page XI) by what right I felt justified in doing so. I argued that where our typefaces depart from the style of "second-order" uncial manuscripts (such as almost all those written after the seventh century), they actually more closely resemble those older models upon which the later manuscripts themselves were based. This seemed sufficient to me, for I know well that it is impossible to carve and cast new typefaces for the sake of printing every single manuscript or fragment. Nevertheless, so that the differences within that very similarity might not remain hidden, so-called facsimiles <term>facsimile: an exact copy or reproduction of a manuscript page</term> engraved on stone <note>This refers to lithography, a printing process using stone plates.</note> have been added to the published texts of the manuscripts. If any sensible men can offer better results by following—as I highly recommend—the example I gave with the *Codex Friderico-Augustanus* (which was engraved entirely on stone), they will have done a most welcome service. On the other hand, I care nothing for unfair critics who neither love to be taught and take much more pleasure in the freedom to find fault than in the memory of good works.

May God, the Best and Greatest, grant that all my labors concerning the sacred text, however imperfect, may truly benefit the Church that cherishes divine truth. No greater praise is sought for those works, nor for myself, than to be approved by Christ.

->I was writing this in Leipzig at the end of October in the year of salvation 1856.<-

# I.

The first notice of the discovery of this palimpsest <term>palimpsest: a manuscript where the original text was scraped off so the parchment could be reused for a new text</term> treasure was given in the year 1848 by the discoverer himself, the most illustrious William Cureton, in the preface to the book he titled: *The festal letters of Athanasius, discovered in an ancient Syriac version, and edited by William Cureton.* There, he relates the history of the written books brought to the British Museum in the years 1838 and 1847 from the Coptic Monastery of Nitria, which is named after Saint Mary, Mother of God <note>original: "St. Mariae Deiparae"</note>. This collection and purchase of manuscripts, by which so many treasures of antiquity of the highest value were rescued from destruction and handed over for the use of literature, is a source of glory to the British name itself. Among those books is a vast quantity of Syriac manuscripts. Within their number, a distinguished place is held by that which—

*) Furthermore, the typographical perfection of Volume I found such great praise among the illustrious judges at the celebrated competition of arts and craftsmen recently held in Paris, that the printers I just named were honored with the first prize for works of that kind.

<meta>This page continues the technical introduction to the manuscript collection. <page-type>text</page-type>
<sig>xv</sig>

...contains the writings of Severus, the Bishop of Antioch, who was famous from the Eutychian heresy <note>The Eutychian heresy, or Monophysitism, was a 5th-century theological dispute regarding whether Christ had one nature or two. Severus was a major figure in this movement.</note>. Not only are the Syriac books it contains unpublished—and the Greek language in which they were first written long since lost—but beneath the Syriac text (which William Cureton*) judged to have been written at the end of the ninth or the beginning of the tenth century), there survive most precious remains of three Greek parchment books that were erased with a sponge to allow for a second writing <note>This is a "palimpsest," a manuscript where the original text was washed or scraped off so the expensive parchment could be reused for a new text.</note>. When William Cureton noticed these remains and examined them a bit more closely, he realized with great pleasure that one set belonged to the Gospel of Luke, another (and these quite extensive) to Homer's *Iliad*, and the third to some book by Euclid. After briefly explaining all of these, he added two specimens of the text: one from Luke (1:69 to 77) and the other from Homer. At that time, that most learned man intended to publish the fragments of Luke as well as those of Homer. Later, however, pressed by the care of handling other books, especially Syriac ones, he published only the Homer from those Greek palimpsests. I first saw the other part—that is, the fragments of Luke—in the autumn of 1849. At that time, though I strongly desired to work on publishing them, Cureton had not yet abandoned his plan to do so; nevertheless, with the constant goodwill he showed toward me, he allowed me to use the various readings he had already transcribed and to examine the codex <note term="codex">An ancient book composed of handwritten pages bound together, rather than a scroll.</note> myself. I mentioned this in my editions of the New Testament in 1850 and 1854. However, when I learned from Cureton’s letters that he was so occupied with other labors that he no longer planned to publish the Greek palimpsest remains of Luke, I made an effort to return to London as soon as possible. When this happened, in early 1855, I considered nothing more important than to investigate the Greek of Luke lying hidden beneath the Syriac text of Severus. The labor of investigation was no small task, especially since the sky was much more often cloudy than clear, and the rules of the British Museum did not allow faded letters to be restored anywhere by pouring chemical solutions <note>In the 19th century, scholars often used chemicals like "reagents" to briefly make faded ink visible, but this often permanently stained or damaged the parchment.</note>. Nonetheless, I succeeded in reading almost everything—except for a few lines of one page (see page 85 below)—and only rarely did any doubt remain about the true reading of the manuscript.

Now I must briefly describe the technical arrangement of the codex. The Greek book, which seems to have once contained only the Gospel of Luke, was originally in a quarto format <note term="quarto">A format where the parchment sheets are folded twice to create four leaves (eight pages).</note>; when it was converted to an octavo format <note term="octavo">A smaller format where sheets are folded three times to create eight leaves.</note> by the Syriac scribe, damage was done—mostly by cutting away the margins of the parchment and, in places, the text itself. The parchment does not excel in thinness compared to other manuscripts of such great antiquity. The ink is brown, not of a yellowish color. The text, as accurately represented in this edition, is written in two columns on each page; twenty-five lines are placed in each column. The handwriting is in **uncial script**, of the type we know was mostly used in the fifth, sixth, and seventh centuries. It therefore features square and rounded letter forms, full and not yet stretched in height. Indeed, the size of the individual letters slightly exceeds that found in most of the oldest manuscripts, even in the fragments of Homer discovered in the same codex of Severus. In this respect, it most closely resembles four manuscripts, three of which I have edited myself and the fourth of which I am about to edit. These three are: 1. the Gospel remains on parchment...

---

*) See the previously cited work, page XXX: 
> „in all probability the volume formed a part of the collection which was deposited in the
monastery of St. Mary Deipara by Moses of Nisibis, the Abbot, when he returned from Bagdad in A. D. 931. This
would refer the date of the transcript to the end of the ninth, or the beginning of the tenth century at the latest.“

<meta>This page continues Tischendorf’s technical analysis of the manuscript's age. On the previous page, he introduced the discovery of the Greek palimpsest fragments of Luke, Homer, and Euclid beneath a Syriac text. Here, he compares the script of this "London palimpsest" to other famous manuscripts to determine its date, settling on the 6th century.</meta>

<page-num>XVI</page-num>

...written in silver and gold on purple parchment, which we published in 1846 in *Unpublished Sacred Monuments* <note>Original Latin title: *Monumenta sacra inedita*</note> under the names of the Cotton (I), Caesarean (N), and Vatican (Γ) codices; 2. two palimpsest fragments of Luke published by us in the first volume of the *New Collection of Unpublished Sacred Monuments* and represented by a facsimile in what they call Plate II, number IV; 3. other palimpsest fragments of Luke published in the same volume, a specimen of which we displayed engraved in stone in *Sacred and Profane Anecdotes* <note>Original Latin: *Anecdota sacra et profana*</note> on Plate III, number VI. To these is added another of the Gospel codices found in the Wolfenbüttel palimpsest books <note>Wolfenbüttel is a city in Germany famous for its ducal library (the Herzog August Bibliothek), which houses many ancient "palimpsests"—manuscripts where the original text was erased and written over.</note>, a specimen of which was shown by Knittel on Plate III of that book, in which he first published the Gothic fragments of Ulphilas <note>Ulphilas was a 4th-century bishop who translated the Bible into the Gothic language; his work is the primary source for that extinct Germanic tongue.</note> along with various other literary monuments.*) Furthermore, the Laudian Codex of the Acts of the Apostles (in Greek and Latin) can be compared, as can, if I am not mistaken, the Dublin palimpsest of Matthew.

Now the question is whether this letter size carries weight in defining the age of the codex. Against someone concluding a later age from this, it can be argued that some of the oldest Latin codices—among which the St. Gall palimpsest fragments of Virgil are prominent, written in the third or fourth century—are very conspicuous for their great letter size. But I do not know if the rule for Greek and Latin writing was different in this matter. Indeed, the oldest Greek parchment monuments we know—such as the Codex Frederico-Augustanus, the Vatican Bible codex <note>Known today as Codex Vaticanus (B), one of the oldest and most important Greek Bibles.</note>, the Sarravianus Pentateuch, the Vatican fragments of Dio Cassius, and the palimpsest fragments of the New Testament that I brought from the East of the first, second, and third types, and the Cotton Genesis—all have a certain medium letter size, hardly different from the style used in papyrus rolls. 

On the contrary, among those codices which show signs of a later age because of the very style of their <term>uncial</term> <note>Uncial letters are a type of large, rounded capital script used by Greek and Latin scribes between the 4th and 8th centuries AD.</note> letters—which already deviate in various ways from the full, classical forms—most surpass the older ones in the size of their writing. Nor should it be overlooked that in the London palimpsest of Luke, examples of "elongated uncial" script <note>Original French: "onciale altérée," referring to a style where letters are stretched or distorted from their original proportions.</note> are not entirely missing. Such is found on page 48, column 2, line 16, in the word *telesthē* <gloss>completed</gloss>, likewise on page 66, column 2, line 8, the *ou* in *anthrōpou* <gloss>of the man</gloss> <note>The OCR shows the Greek abbreviation for "man" (anthropos).</note>, page 70, column 2, line 11, the *ō* in *autō* <gloss>to him</gloss>, and especially the inscription of the chapters. These chapter headings might seem to have been written by a different hand for that very reason, were it not certain that similar things were done elsewhere in inscriptions or additions of that kind. We observed a notable example of this in the other Wolfenbüttel <term>palimpsests</term> <note>A palimpsest is a "recycled" manuscript where the original text was scraped off to make room for a new one.</note> mentioned above. But we do not wish to pursue these matters too minutely now. Taking into account everything relevant here, the codex in question seems to me to be no later than the sixth century; however, I think it was written in the sixth rather than the fifth.

The punctuation of the text is done with a simple dot. This does not have a varying meaning, whether it is placed at the top of the preceding letter (which is what usually happens) or—as we have rarely observed (see the example in the stone-engraved plate)—occupies a slightly lower position; for this reason, it is placed in the same spot everywhere in our edition. Often, however, a dot is not placed even where it is most customary to do so, especially where a section ends and is followed by an initial letter; just as the chapters displayed in the index sometimes have a dot added, but more often do not. Since this matter often leaves no doubt, in those places where we could not clearly identify a dot, we did not...

---

*) We ourselves will repeat the remains of both Gospel palimpsests in the third volume of *Unpublished Sacred Monuments*, not fearing that we misuse the name "unpublished." For Knittel clearly did not read many pages, and left even more with gaps; furthermore, in those pages which he did publish as having been read by himself, there are many things that ought to have been read more correctly and edited more accurately.

<page-num>XVII</page-num>
<sig>c</sig>

...we have published: although in palimpsest books <note>A palimpsest is a manuscript where the original writing was scraped off so the parchment could be reused. Tischendorf is explaining how hard it is to see small marks like punctuation on such recycled surfaces.</note> it is often very difficult to tell whether the scribe added a dot or not, and therefore it is easy to be mistaken. Examples of inappropriate punctuation are rare, such as on page 7, column 1, line 23: which can itself be compared to the way that in later codices, such as in the Bodleian Codex of Genesis, a mark is usually placed after relative pronouns.

Just as we have carefully noted the placement or omission of the dot, we have done the same for the double dots <term>diaeresis</term> placed over the letters iota (ι) and upsilon (υ): it is probable that these were placed by the scribe with greater consistency than we have shown in our edition, although we have observed the same word written sometimes with dots and sometimes without in many codices. The same applies to the apostrophe, as seen in the names *Elizabeth* <note>original: ελισαβετ'</note> and *Capernaum* <note>original: καφαρναουμ'</note>.

In addition to the scribal abbreviations <note>Latin: compendia scripturae</note> accurately reproduced in our edition, the syllable *mou* <note>The Greek possessive pronoun meaning "of me" or "my".</note> is written a few times as a contraction: <note>ligature of the Greek letters mu, omicron, and upsilon, with the omicron and upsilon stacked above the mu</note>. This occurs about five times: page 29, col. 1, line 5 in the word *deserts* <note>original: ερημους</note>, likewise in the word *my* <note>original: μου</note> on page 47, col. 2, line 14; page 72, col. 1, line 1; page 77, col. 2, line 2; and page 91, col. 2, line 15.

The size of the initial letters can be seen in the plate engraved in stone <note>Tischendorf included lithographic plates—early "faxes" or photos of the manuscript—in his printed book so scholars could see the handwriting for themselves.</note>, although even larger ones may be found. Indeed, here and there they have a somewhat smaller form, seemingly to signify a minor distinction in the text. This is generally the case on page 10, col. 2, line 12; page 12, col. 2, line 22; page 25, col. 2, line 19; page 29, col. 2, line 12; page 37, col. 2, line 10; page 40, col. 2, line 24; page 48, col. 2, line 8; page 51, col. 2, line 20; page 52, col. 1, line 23; and page 83, col. 2, line 22.

These are the details regarding the script of the codex, which we have placed before the eyes of learned men by means of a specimen—albeit a brief one—engraved in stone, after we took the greatest care to reproduce the nature of the palimpsest codices in the second plate of Volume I. (The same will be done in Volume III.) Furthermore, six very beautiful specimens of that same Syriac codex from which we took the Gospel of Luke—specifically from those leaves containing fragments of Homer—were provided by the most venerable William Cureton <note>William Cureton (1808–1864) was a famous British Orientalist and priest who worked with these same manuscripts at the British Museum.</note> in the book: *Fragments of the Iliad of Homer from a Syriac palimpsest*. Printed by order of the Trustees of the British Museum, by Rich. Taylor, 1851.

For one asking what the homeland of the codex was, one must think of either Asia or Africa. It makes use of what they call "Alexandrian" forms (such as the spelling *lempsontai* <note>original: λημψονται; a specific spelling of "they shall receive" common in Egyptian/Alexandrian manuscripts</note> in Luke 20:47), but not consistently; just like the *Codex Ephraemi Rescriptus*—with which it agrees more than any other in the general character of its text—it preserves both the spellings *Kapernaum* and *Kapharnaum*. Scribal errors are rare, if you except the interchangeable use of certain vowels like *ai* and *e*, or *ei* and *i*, along with a few similar cases, and the occasional confusion of *o* and *ō* <note>This refers to the common "itacism" in Greek manuscripts where vowels that sounded the same were swapped by tired scribes.</note>.

The "Ammonian Sections" <note>An early system for dividing the Gospels into numbered sections for easier reading and study.</note> written in the margin caused us much trouble. Just as in the *Codex Ephraemi*, the Dublin palimpsest of Matthew, our own palimpsest fragments of the New Testament, and other very ancient codices, they do not have the numbers of the "Eusebian Canons" <note>The Eusebian Canons were a cross-reference system used to find the same story in different Gospels. Their absence here suggests the manuscript is very old.</note> added to them. It often happens that one doubts whether those numbers are simply not visible enough or were omitted by the scribe. We believe several were actually omitted in the final chapters of the Gospel. Besides the Ammonian Sections, in a few places we found chapter numbers written in the upper margin; compare page 34 <note>number 29</note>, page 54 <note>number 56</note>, and as will be supplemented in the commentary, page 32 <note>number 27</note> and page 80 <note>number 73</note>. It is very likely that many more such numbers were cut off along with the very tops of the margins <note>When manuscripts were rebound in later centuries, binders often trimmed the edges of the parchment, accidentally cutting off marginal notes.</note>.

<summary>Tischendorf describes the technical scribal features of the manuscript, including punctuation, ligatures, and vowel variations, while comparing it to other famous codices like the Codex Ephraemi. He also notes that the manuscript contains early Gospel division systems (Ammonian Sections) but lacks the Eusebian Canon numbers, which helps date and categorize the text.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Tischendorf’s technical description of the manuscript's physical state. On the previous page, he discussed punctuation and shorthand marks; here, he explains the arrangement of the recycled pages and begins a leaf-by-leaf analysis of specific readings and corrections.</meta>

<page-num>XVIII</page-num>

It is not necessary to indicate the order in which the individual Greek fragments follow one another if we were to follow the Syriac text. Indeed, all the leaves—each of which consists of two Syriac pages in octavo format making up a single Greek page in quarto format—have been separated by the care of the library supervisors and placed in the order required by the continuity of the Greek text. All the fragments of the Gospel of Luke are contained within the first ninety leaves of the Syriac text. Furthermore, as is common in very old palimpsest <term>palimpsest: a manuscript where the original text was scraped off so the parchment could be reused</term> codices, there is a great inequality in the pages of every leaf. We have long and often taught that this arises from the fact that the side of the parchment which once touched the flesh was rendered much softer than the side which had been covered by hair. In these softer parts of the parchment, the difficulty of reading the text is usually much greater than in the others.

It remains for us to explain certain places in detail as we survey the individual leaves.

**Leaf I recto (page 3).** The first letter of the inscription, K <note>original: κ (kappa)</note>, as well as the final letters AIA <note>original: αια (alpha-iota-alpha)</note>, have been trimmed away along with the greater part of the letter L <note>original: λ (lambda)</note>. Furthermore, several letters of the inscription, especially both instances of O <note>omicron</note> and the E <note>epsilon</note>, do not have a round shape but rather a drawn-out or oblong form. A very similar pattern is found in the index of chapters in the other Wolfenbüttel codex <note>Latin: Guelpherbytano</note> of the Gospels. We have already spoken of this matter above on page XVI. The first three lines of Column 1 were written in red ink <note>Latin: minio</note>. Because of this, they have completely faded away. The numbers 1, 2, and 3 <note>original: α', β', γ'</note> which were added in the margin, as well as the number 10 <note>original: ι'</note> at line 23, have been lost along with the margin itself. A dot is rarely placed at the end of chapters; certainly, we have not included one except where we could perceive it with our own eyes.

**Leaf I verso (page 4).** In column 2, line 1, the mark "⌋" which we published after the word "of the" <note>original: του</note> seems to be the first part of the letter K <note>kappa</note>, so that it was likely written as "of the Lord" <note>original: ΚΥ (abbreviation for Kyriou)</note> rather than "of Jesus" <note>original: ΙΥ (abbreviation for Iesou)</note>. In the same place at line 14, we have published the number 72 <note>original: οβ'</note>, for it certainly appears so. The ends of lines 23, 24, and 25 in the same place have been trimmed off.

**Leaf II recto (page 5).** The beginnings of several lines are trimmed away. We have enclosed the reconstructions of these missing parts in brackets everywhere. The number 82 <note>original: πβ'</note> mostly survives because, as usually happens with these numbers, it occupies a place slightly higher than the initial letter that follows it.

**Leaf III recto (page 7).** In column 1, the first three lines were written in red ink. Consequently, what we have published in this place cannot be considered absolutely certain. We have already spoken of the dot placed after the words "concerning which" <note>original: περι ων</note> in line 23. Similarly, in Codex L <note>a Greek manuscript of Luke and John kept at Oxford</note>, there is a comma in the same place. In line 25, we have published "very much" <note>original: ΛΙΑΝ (lian)</note> as the last word. It certainly seemed to be written in this manner rather than "smooth" <note>original: ΛΕΙΑ (leia)</note>, though I had my doubts. In column 2, line 24, a dot, if it was perhaps written, certainly does not appear.

**Leaf III verso (page 8).** In column 2, line 18, the letter "s" <note>sigma</note> in the name *Zacharias* has been erased.

**Leaf IV recto (page 9).** In column 2, line 3, the word "fearless" <note>original: αφοβοσ (aphobos)</note> appears: the same error is found in Codex G <note>an Oxford codex of the four Gospels</note> and others.*)

**Leaf IV verso (page 10).** This page has the softer part of the parchment; hence the whole of column 1 is quite difficult to read. In line 3, it is not entirely certain whether "mercies" <note>original: σπλα</note> was written, or whether—as becomes probable by comparing page 23, column 2, lines 1 and 2—it was written as "tender mercies" <note>original: σπλαγχ or σπλαχ</note>. However, as this word is read here and there in ancient codices with the "g" <note>gamma</note> omitted, I thought it should be published that way. Line 14, occupying roughly the middle of the page where the Syriac codex was folded, is almost entirely [unclear] across both columns...

---

*) Stars have been painted here and there to indicate that this commentary on individual passages should be consulted.
**) Furthermore, in the specimen of the text which the illustrious William Cureton published in the preface of the book *The Festal Letters of Athanasius* (p. XXXII), in column 1, line 14, "joy all" <note>original: χαιροσ παν</note> was transcribed by error instead of "from the hand of all" <note>original: χειροσ παντῶ</note>; likewise in column 2, line 25, "he will please" <note>original: αρεσει</note> instead of "he releases" <note>original: αφιεσι</note>.

On the previous page, he described the physical condition of the first leaf and the general challenges of reading palimpsest parchment; here, he moves through Leaves V to XIX, noting specific spelling errors, difficult readings due to damage, and instances where ink from the reverse side of the page shows through the parchment.</meta>

<page-num>XIX</page-num>
<sig>c*</sig>

...has perished. **Folio V recto (page 11)**, column 2, line 4, "they returned" <note>original: ὑπετρεψαν (hypetreyan)</note>: it is written this way in the codex, omitting the letter S <note>original: σ (sigma)</note> instead of the correct form "they returned" <note>original: ὑπεστρεψαν (hypestreyan)</note>. **Folio V verso (page 12)**, column 1, is extremely difficult to read. **Folio VI recto (page 13)**: The things written on this page could hardly be read at all except when held up to the light. Column 1, line 6, "the" <note>original: τυ (ty)</note>: the codex mistakenly has this instead of the correct form <note>original: τω (tō)</note>. At the end of line 14, it is not sufficiently clear whether "ChON" <note>original: ΧΟ$\overline{\text{N}}$</note> was written with an overline or simply as "ChON" <note>original: ΧΟΝ</note>. Similarly, the last syllable of line 23 might be doubted. **Folio VII recto (page 15)**, column 1, line 4, "upon which" <note>original: εφ ω (eph hō)</note>: so it stands. In the same place, line 16, "let us see" <note>original: ιδωμεν (idōmen)</note>: so it stands. In the same place, line 22, the second E <note>epsilon</note> in the word "to serve" <note>original: λευειν (leuein)</note> is a little paler than the other letters: hence one might believe it was erased by a corrector. However, in the passage that follows in column 2, line 8, the same letter in "you serve" <note>original: λευεις (leueis)</note> remained untouched. Column 2, line 17, "I murmured" <note>original: εγογγυζᾱ (egongyza)</note> with an overline: so it stands. **Folio VII verso (page 16)**, column 1: the first ten lines are especially difficult to read. Nevertheless, it seemed they should be published in that manner. **Folio VIII recto (page 17)** is quite difficult to read, especially the first column. Column 2, line 12, "second" <note>original: δευτερω (deuterō)</note> is certain. **Folio VIII verso (page 18)**, column 2, line 19, "if in" <note>original: ει εν (ei en)</note>, although placed as a conjecture, nevertheless fits the available space very well. In line 21, however, it cannot be determined whether "he heals" <note>original: θεραπευει (therapeuei)</note> or "he will heal" <note>original: θεραπευσι (therapeusi) or θεραπευσει (therapeusei)</note> was written. **Folio IX recto (page 19)**, column 1, line 18, it is doubtful whether "pei" <note>original: πει</note> or "pi" <note>original: πι</note> is written. Likewise, the final letters in lines 22 and 25 are not clearly visible. In column 2, at line 12, we have added the number 49 <note>original: $\mu\overline{\theta}$ (mu-theta)</note>: but it is scarcely visible. From line 19 onward, everything is very difficult to read. Because the passage is so obscure, we have published "for according to these things" <note>original: κατα ταυτα γαρ (kata tauta gar)</note> not without hesitation. Certainly, however, this reading is much more probable than the one which follows on page 20, column 1, lines 15 and 16, "for according to the same things" <note>original: κατα τα αυτα γαρ (kata ta auta gar)</note> (the point <term>point: a punctuation mark</term> which you would expect before it was not placed). **Folio IX verso (page 20)**, column 2, line 11, the number 53 <note>original: $\nu\overline{\gamma}$ (nu-gamma)</note> was restored by the original hand from the number 52 <note>original: $\nu\overline{\beta}$ (nu-beta)</note>. In the same place, line 25, we published the number 54 <note>original: $\nu\overline{\delta}$ (nu-delta)</note>, but a trace of it can barely be made out. **Folio X recto (page 21)** excels most of the other leaves in the quality of its writing. **Folio XI recto (page 23)**, column 1, line 13, a Z <note>original: ζ (zeta)</note> seems to be hidden under the S <note>original: σ (sigma)</note> in "he approached" <note>original: εγγισεν (engisen)</note>, but that is showing through from the reverse side of the page. In the same place, line 25, we published "he remained" <note>original: εμινεν (eminen)</note>: for so it truly seemed. Nevertheless, it cannot be said with certainty whether it was written this way or as "he remained" <note>original: εμεινεν (emeinen)</note>, especially since in the same spot on the reverse side of the page, the letter E <note>original: $\in$ (epsilon)</note>, written with strong strokes, shows through. In the same place, column 2, line 4, "and having cried out" <note>original: και κραξας (kai kraxas)</note>: the codex has it so. **Folio XIV verso (page 30)**, column 2, line 7, "rysnē" <note>original: ρυσνη</note>: so it stands. **Folio XV recto (page 31)**, the middle of the line, number 13, has almost entirely perished where the parchment <term>parchment: animal skin prepared for writing</term> is torn. **Folio XV verso (page 32)**, supply the number 27 <note>original: $\overline{\text{KZ}}$ (kappa-zeta)</note>, which was added in the top margin between the two columns to indicate the chapter. See page 54. **Folio XVI recto (page 33)**, column 1, line 20, the codex has "mi" <note>original: μι</note> instead of "mē" <note>original: μη - "not"</note>. Furthermore, this leaf also (pages 33 and 34) is torn through the middle, which is why line 13 of both columns on both pages had to be enclosed in brackets. Again, **Folio XVI verso (page 34)** has the number 29 <note>original: $\overline{\text{KO}}$ (kappa-theta)</note> written above the first column to indicate Chapter 29. **Folio XVII recto (page 35)**, column 1, line 5, half of the letter Ps <note>original: $\Psi$ (psi)</note> has been cut away. In the same place, line 19, "he lost" <note>original: απωλεσι (apōlesi)</note>: so it stands; likewise line 20, "he might lose" <note>original: απωλεση (apōlesē)</note>; by contrast, column 2, line 5 correctly has "having lost" <note>original: απολεσας (apolesas)</note>. **Folio XVIII recto (page 37)**, column 1, lines 1 and 2, the question is whether "he said" <note>original: ειπεν (eipen)</note> or "he was saying" <note>original: ελεγεν (elegen)</note> was written. To me, indeed, searching the place repeatedly, it seemed that "he was saying" was read rather than "he said." In the same place, column 2, line 23, we published "they met" <note>original: συνηντησαν (synēntēsan)</note>, as it is in the codex. **Folio XVIII verso (page 38)**, column 1, line 11, "he takes" <note>original: λαμβαννι (lambanni)</note>: so it stands. **Folio XIX verso (page 40)**, column 1, line 2, "decho-" <note>original: δεχω with a line above</note>: so it certainly seems entirely, and that fits for "to enter" <note>original: εισερχησθαι (eiserchesthai)</note>. (In lines 12 and 13) the words "to enter" <note>original: εισερχεσθαι (eiserchesthai)</note> and "they receive" <note>original: δεχονται (dechontai)</note> preceded. In the same place, column 1, line 10: "old" <note>original: πολαιος (polaios)</note>; thus it stands. In the same place, lines 17 and 18, which we have published, are extremely difficult to read. **Folio XX verso (page 42)**: This page is among the most difficult; in particular, lines 7–9 and 18–25 of column 1 are hidden, as are the first eight lines of column 2. **Folio XXI verso (page 44)**, column 2, line 7: "Spirit" <note>original: $\overline{\text{ΠNA}}$ (pna)</note>; so it stands as we published it, not as it is usually written elsewhere, "Spirits" <note>original: $\overline{\text{ΠNATA}}$ (pnata)</note>.

**Folio XXII recto (page 45)**, column 2, line 10: "he shall confess" <note>original: ομολογησι (homologēsi)</note>; thus it stands, I could read it no other way.

**Folio XXIII verso (page 48)**, column 2, line 5: "more abundantly" <note>original: περισσωτερ[ον] (perissoteron)</note>; so it stands, as we published. The letters E, E, S, and Th in the word "should be finished" <note>original: τελεσθη (telesthē)</note> have an oblong rather than a round shape; but I do not know whether the scribe of the <term>codex</term> <note>an ancient manuscript in book form</note> himself changed the shape due to a lack of space. **Folio XXIV recto (page 49)**, column 1, line 2: "let us eat" <note>original: εφαγωμεν (ephagōmen)</note>; thus it stands.

**Folio XXV recto (page 51)**, column 1, line 4: "recompense" <note>original: ανταποδωμα (antapodōma)</note>; thus it stands.

**Folio XXV verso (page 52)**, column 2, lines 22–24: the words "and compel them to come in" <note>original: και αναγκασον εισελθειν (kai anagkason eiselthein)</note> are extremely difficult to read; yet I seemed to discern them sufficiently.

**Folio XXVI verso (page 54)**: 56 <note>original: $\overline{\nu\varsigma}$ (ns)</note> is again the chapter number; compare the note above for Folio XVI.

**Folio XXVII recto (page 55)**, column 2, line 17: "I go" <note>original: πορευομαι (poreuomai)</note>; thus it stands; to make this sufficiently clear, it had to be viewed against the light.

**Folio XXVII verso (page 56)**, column 1, line 1: The words that the scribe wrongly omitted before "your son" <note>original: υιος σου (hyios sou)</note> due to <term>homoeoteleuton</term> <note>a common scribal error where the eye skips from one word to another with a similar ending, omitting the text in between</note> were later supplied in the upper margin in <term>uncial</term> <note>large, capital-letter script</note> letters of roughly the ninth century. Part of this addition, placed at the very top of the margin, has perished; but what remains is read in this manner:

> ]arising[ <note>original: ]αναστας[</note>
> him his father and was moved with compassion; and running he] <note>original: αυτον ο πηρ αυτου κι εσπλαγχνησθ[η· κι δραμων ε]</note>
> fell upon his neck and [kissed him.] <note>original: πεπεσεν επι τον τραχηλον αυτου κι κατ[εφιλησεν αυτον˙]</note>
> And the son said to him: Father, I have sinned against heaven [and before you] <note>original: ειπεν δε αυτω ο υς˙ περ ημαρτον εις τον ουνον [ν κι ενωπιον σου]</note>
> and no longer <note>original: κι ουκετι</note>
> am I worthy <note>original: ειμι αξιος</note>
> to be called <note>original: κληθηναι</note>

For the rest, the first five lines of column 1 are very difficult to read, as are the final ones, especially lines 23, 24, and 25. What we have provided is certainly very probable. In line 25: "I shall destroy" <note>original: απολω (apolō)</note>; it seemed to be written entirely this way. The letters "los" <note>original: λος</note> which follow are certain.

**Folio XXVIII verso (page 58)**, column 1, line 14: lost due to a tear.

**Folio XXIX recto (page 59)**, column 2, line 10, "it may fail" <note>original: εκλιπη (eklipē)</note>: it seemed to be written thus rather than the more common spelling "it may fail" <note>original: εκλειπη (ekleipē)</note>. **Folio XXX recto (page 61)**, column 1, line 14, [204] <note>original: σδ (sd)—Greek numerals where sigma equals 200 and delta equals 4</note>: we have published it thus more by <term>conjecture</term> <note>A scholar’s "conjecture" is an educated guess about a missing or unreadable word based on context or similar manuscripts.</note>; for the letters do not appear sufficiently.

**Folio XXXIV recto (page 69)**, column 1, line 11: "and of some" <note>original: και τινος (kai tinos)</note>: so it is. Afterwards, in line 13 of both columns, [repay] <note>original: αποδι (apodi)</note> and [me with] <note>original: μα με (ma me)</note> were lost through a tear in the <term>membrane</term> <note>The parchment or animal skin used as a writing surface.</note>. In the same place, line 17: "them" <note>original: αυτους (autous)</note>: so it is. In the same place, column 2, line 10: **Jerusalem** <note>original: **ΙΑΗΜ̅** (Iāēm)—a <term>nomen sacrum</term> or sacred abbreviation for the city</note> we published with care, but it might also have been **ΙΑΗΜ'**.

**Folio XXXIV verso (page 70)**, line 13: in both columns, the parts we included in brackets were lost because the parchment was torn. In the same place, column 2, line 5: the letter "n" <note>original: ν (nu)</note> in "they traded" <note>original: πραγματευσαντο (pragmateusanto)</note> is sufficiently clear and certain. Conversely, in line 3, it is not sufficiently clear whether "he might know" <note>original: γνοι (gnoi)</note> or "he might know" <note>original: γνω (gnō)</note> was written. In the same place, line 8: the letter "s" <note>original: σ (sigma)</note> was written above the line, as we published. But it seems to have been written thus by the "first hand" <note>The original scribe, rather than a later corrector.</note>. Compare above, Folio 11 recto, column 1, line 23, where "anciently" <note>original: πωλαιωσ (pōlaiōs)</note> is read.

**Folio XXXVIII verso (page 78)**: This page not only contains the softer part of the parchment but is also in poor condition. In the first column, line 24, "head of" <note>original: κεφαλην γο (kephalēn go)</note>: thus exactly in the manuscript.

**Folio XLII recto (page 85)**: This page, especially the second column, is more difficult to read than all the other leaves. Everything could easily be made clearer by using <term>chemical reagents</term> <note>In the 19th century, scholars often used chemicals like ammonium hydrosulphide to temporarily darken faded ink. Unfortunately, these chemicals often eventually turned the parchment black, permanently damaging the manuscript.</note>; for it is not the softer part of the parchment, where the use of chemicals is often destructive. The things we transcribed in type in the first column are hardly doubtful; in line 17, I was wavering between "And he entered the" <note>original: Εισηλθεν δε ο (Eisēlthen de ho)</note> and "And he entered" <note>original: Εισηλθεν δε (Eisēlthen de)</note>, but the "the" <note>original: ο</note> seemed to be omitted. In the second column, the following could be given as certain: line 1, "he talked with" <note>original: συνελαλη (synelalē)</note>; line 9, "they put to him" <note>original: θεντο αυτω (thento autō)</note>; likewise lines 13 and 14. The things we published as noted on the upper margin have indeed smaller letter forms, but they [show] equal status with the text...

They possess an elegance such that they can easily be attributed to the scribe of the <term>codex</term> <note>A codex is an ancient book format with handwritten pages bound together, rather than a scroll.</note> himself. **Folio 43 verso (page 88)**: across that entire page, the writing of which is well preserved in places, no numbers could be recognized, although we returned to search for them repeatedly. In the same place, column 2, line 1, "but" <note>original: αλ[λα] (alla)</note>: it is most probable that it was written thus; but the cut-off part is based on <term>conjecture</term> <note>An educated guess by a scholar to fill in missing or unreadable text based on context.</note>. **Folio 44 recto (page 89)**, column 1, line 13, "He began" <note>original: Ηρξατο (Ērxato)</note>: written as we have published it, not "they began" <note>original: ηρξαντο (ērxanto)</note>; likewise "to give" <note>original: διδωναι (didōnai)</note> in line 22 and "teaching" <note>original: διδασκον (didaskon)</note> in column 2, line 21. Furthermore, neither throughout this entire page nor the one that follows do the <term>Ammonian numbers</term> <note>A system of numbering the sections of the Four Gospels, created by Ammonius of Alexandria in the 3rd century.</note> appear to have been added. **Folio 44 verso (page 90)**, column 2, line 1, "he hoped" <note>original: ηλπιζεν (hēlpizen)</note>: what we have published is certain because the lowest part of the letter **Z** remains. Likewise, in the following sections, we have published several letters which, based on the surviving parts, cannot be called into doubt. Column 2, line 24, we published "bright" <note>original: λαμπ[ραν] (lampran)</note>: for it is much more probable that it was written thus, given the proportion of the space, than "bright" followed by a numeral <note>original: λαμπραν α̅</note>. In the last line, however, we have not indicated the cut-off letters. **Folio 45 recto and verso (pages 91 and 92)**: on both of those pages, no numbers could be found except for two which are clearly visible. **Folio 45 verso (page 92)**, column 1, lines 10 and 11, "I set before" <note>original: παρατιθειμι (paratitheimi)</note>: the ending "set before" <note>original: θειμι (theimi)</note> is certain; nor do I doubt the letter that precedes it, although at first glance I wavered between **H** and **I**. The same must be said for "stand" <note>original: στηθι (stēthi)</note> in column 2, line 7. In the same place, line 21, we have published **JOSEPH** <note>original: IΩCHΦ</note> and not **JOSEPH’** <note>original: IΩCHΦ’</note>. However, the spot where an apostrophe is usually placed is completely covered by a second layer of writing.

### II.

## RELICS RESCUED FROM THE FIRE OF THE MOST FAMOUS COTTON CODEX.

The history of that Cotton Codex is quite remarkable. We learn how it came to England and into that very library from which it still takes its name (although it has long since become part of the British Museum) from a note that Richard James, once the keeper of the Cotton Library, had placed at the front of the codex. Henry Owen later included this note in the preface of the *Collation of Grabe* which he published.*) It reads as follows:
> "A book of Genesis illuminated with very beautiful figures, and written in most ancient capital letters about fourteen or fifteen centuries ago; which two Greek Bishops brought with them from Philippi to England in the time of Henry VIII, offering it as a gift to him, and claiming that it was received from an ancient tradition that it once belonged to the master, Saint Origen. Queen Elizabeth, of happy memory, placed it in the Royal Library..."

---

*) The title of the book is: *A Collation of the Cotton Codex of Genesis with the Roman Edition*. Made long ago by the most distinguished man Johannes Ernestus Grabe, and now finally published with the greatest care by Henry Owen, M.D., Fellow of the Royal Society, Rector of the Church of St. Olave. London, 1778.

<summary>This page concludes detailed paleographical notes on manuscript Folios 43–45 before beginning a historical account of the "Cotton Genesis," a 5th-century manuscript gifted to Henry VIII and later damaged by fire.</summary>
<keywords>paleography, Cotton Genesis, manuscript history, Richard James, Henry Owen, Origen, British Museum, Ammonian numbers, Greek bishops, Henry VIII</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the historical account of the "Cotton Genesis" (Codex Cottonianus). While the previous page focused on the paleographical details of specific folios and the absence of section numbers, Tischendorf now shifts to the provenance of the manuscript, its near-destruction in the 1731 fire, and his own efforts to transcribe the surviving charred fragments in 1855.</meta>

<page-num>XXIII</page-num>

> “Found in the archives, he gave it to the most illustrious Knight <term>John Fortescue</term> <note>Sir John Fortescue (c. 1531–1607) was a major figure in the court of Elizabeth I and a scholar of Greek.</note>, his instructor in Greek; who, looking out for the benefit of posterity, enriched the **Cotton Library** <note>original: Thesaurum Cottonianum. The famous collection of Sir Robert Cotton, which formed one of the founding collections of the British Museum.</note> with the same.”

Therefore, by this testimony, it becomes clear that even before the middle of the sixteenth century, the Cotton codex had been brought to London and given as a gift to <term>Henry VIII</term>: a fact also confirmed by a more recent note from the authority of the **British Museum** prefixed to the remains of the codex which still survive on the binding. For there it is written:

> “British Museum, Cotton Library, Otho B. VI. Formerly of King Henry VIII.” <note>original: Mus. Brit. bibl. Cotton. Otho B. VI. Olim R. Hen. VIII.</note>

Likewise, <term>James</term> <note>Likely referring to Richard James, the librarian for the Cotton family.</note> testifies by what path that codex arrived in the library of the knight <term>Robert Cotton</term>. While it was there and held in the highest regard, it happened that it was most miserably affected by the disastrous fire that swept through the entire library in the year 1731. <term>Henry Owen</term> provides testimony of this matter on page XIII of the previously cited work in these words:

> “This most precious Codex finally perished in the tragic fire that seized the **Cotton Library** in the year 1731, except for certain pages miserably damaged by the flames (but now lost).” <note>original: Pretiosissimus hic Codex funesto tandem incendio... prorsus interiit.</note>

From this, therefore, it is also understood that the pages rescued from the flames themselves, although damaged, were considered lost at the time <term>Owen</term> was publishing his booklet in the year 1778. Later, however, they were found: indeed, in the **Holmes edition of the Pentateuch** <note>Robert Holmes (1748–1805) was a biblical scholar who began a massive project to collate all manuscripts of the Septuagint, the Greek Old Testament.</note> from the year 1798, in Chapter II under Section I of the preface, those words of <term>Henry Owen</term> in which he says the remains were lost are deliberately omitted, even though the rest is repeated. Once they were found, the most distinguished curators of the British Museum—in whose treasury the Cottonian manuscripts hold a place of no small importance—deemed them worthy of every care. For whatever remains they had found, they restored as best as could be done, enclosing them in paper frames to form a volume, the splendid appearance of which pleasantly deceives the viewer from the outside. 

Yet when the volume is opened, it becomes entirely apparent how much damage the fire inflicted upon the Cottonian parchments. For now, not a single leaf is whole; of many, only tiny parts survive; and of those that do survive, the vast majority are eaten away on all sides by the flames. Because of this, many pages have taken on a charcoal-like color: for indeed, they were very nearly reduced to ashes. Some are torn, others shriveled, others distorted, and others corrupted in various ways. When I saw all these things in London at the beginning of the spring of 1855, although I was not unaware of how great the difficulty of reading them would be, I nevertheless thought it my duty to strain all my powers to accurately read, transcribe, and hand over to the learned world whatever of this so precious monument of sacred letters had escaped destruction. In this matter, I occupied myself for several weeks, laboring without any intermission every day except Sunday, from ten o'clock in the morning until four or, as was soon permitted, five in the afternoon.

Lest I seem to value my own labor more than is just, I must not pass over those who applied their efforts to examining the Cotton codex before it had experienced the ruin of the fire. First, to use the testimony of <term>Holmes</term> in particular, Bishop <term>Walton</term> ordered some part of it to be collated by a learned hand for the use of his **London Polyglot Bible** <note>A "Polyglot" is a Bible containing the text in several languages, often arranged in parallel columns. Brian Walton published his famous version in 1657.</note>. Then, variant readings excerpted from it—

---

<note>
*) The same <term>Walton</term> (compare the Polyglot Bible, Volume VI, in the preface to the notes of <term>Patrick Young</term> <note>original: Patric. Iunii</note>, and also <term>Owen’s</term> booklet in the preface, page VI) noted that the Cotton codex was once a Pentateuch divided into five volumes, but that the later four of these, when they fell into the hands of a certain Frenchman, were never returned to the owner. From where Walton learned this remains entirely hidden, nor has it been confirmed in any way. As for the collation used by Walton—

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the history of the scholarship surrounding the Cotton Genesis, moving from its acquisition and the 1731 fire to the various attempts by scholars to collate and publish its readings before and after the disaster.</meta>

<page-num>XXIV</page-num>

**Thomas Gale** <note>Thomas Gale (1635–1702) was an English classical scholar and antiquarian.</note> made notes in the margin of a certain copy of the Aldine edition, which was later among the books of **Benjamin Kennicott**. Later, **James Ussher**, Primate of Ireland, collated the manuscript with the Frankfurt edition; the Bodleian Library preserves this collation among Ussher’s writings.\*) Likewise, **Magnus Crusius** collated it, and applied his observations to two Göttingen dissertations held in the years 1744 and 1745; indeed, the collation written in Crusius's own hand came into the possession of **Johann Jakob Breitinger** of Zurich. All of these collations were surpassed by the one prepared through the study of **John Ernest Grabe** <note>A Prussian theologian and biblical scholar who settled in England; he is famous for his edition of the Codex Alexandrinus.</note> around the year 1703. Grabe had indeed promised in the prolegomena of his edition of the Codex Alexandrinus (Chapter III, § 6) in the year 1707:

> “that very ancient book of Genesis, older even than the parchment of the Alexandrian manuscript itself, soon to be published in its entirety on its own, or at least the collations of it with the Roman edition, together with its history, for the benefit of the public.” <note>original: „pervetustum illum ipsisque Alexandrinis membranis antiquiorem librum Geneseos propediem integrum seorsim vel saltem illius cum Romana editione collationes praefixa eiusdem historia publicae luci se donaturum.“</note>

But it did not happen that this most celebrated man could publish either the collations or the manuscript itself, as he was overtaken by an untimely death five years later (in the year 1712). In order to fulfill his promise as far as possible, **Henry Owen** in 1778 published that collation of the manuscript—which has already been mentioned above—from Grabe’s papers preserved in the Bodleian Library at Oxford. However, it seemed to us that we should not be satisfied with merely publishing the fragments of the manuscript rescued from the flames, carefully described word for word or rather letter by letter; we also thought that Owen's book, long since very rare, should be used for our edition so that we might extract from it whatever would serve to complete the readings of the manuscript as it once was. Before we do this, we must speak briefly about the condition of the manuscript, its handwriting and age, and likewise about the paintings.

The parchment is generally thin, but it does not equal the delicacy of the **Codex Claromontanus** <note>A 6th-century Greek-Latin manuscript of the Pauline Epistles.</note> or some similar ones. That the form of the manuscript was a quarto <note>original: quaternariam. Referring to the size of the book or its gathering structure.</note>, as we learn from Grabe’s testimony, can still be understood from the remains of several leaves.

The number of lines written on individual pages, where no painting intervenes, fluctuates between 26 (cf. pages 43, 58, 61), 27 (cf. pages 49, 51, 52), and 28 (cf. pages 41, 44, 45, 47, 72). Individual lines, written according to lines drawn straight across the page with a stylus, contain about 27 or, rarely, 30 letters. The uncial letters—whether smaller, as is usually the case, or a little thicker and larger (specimens of both are shown in our plate)—do not deviate from the ancient, pure, round, and square forms. However, initial letters, the kind of which are foreign to our most ancient manuscripts—such as the **Codex Friderico-Augustanus** <note>The name Tischendorf gave to the portion of Codex Sinaiticus he discovered in 1844.</note>, the **Vatican Bible** (Codex Vaticanus), and the **Sarravian Pentateuch**—have already come into use in the Cotton manuscript. The letters **υ** (upsilon) and **ι** (iota), where they are distinguished from the other letters, are usually marked with double dots <note>Similar to a modern diaeresis or umlaut.</note>. (An example of a transverse line is provided in our facsimile.) Likewise, it shares with most similar manuscripts the use of the apostrophe and shorthand abbreviations. In the punctuation of the text, a system of three points is observed; for a point is placed either at the top, the middle, or the bottom of the preceding letter <note>This refers to the "distinctiones" system of Greek punctuation where the height of the dot indicated the length of the pause.</note>.

In terms of grammar, the Cotton manuscript does not differ much from the **Codex Alexandrinus**, the **Codex Ephraemi**, and similar ones.

---

With Grabe, Owen (in the place cited, pages XVI and following) says it is "so defective and imperfect" <note>original: „adeo mancam et imperfectam“</note> "that a repeated comparison of it from head to heel <note>Meaning from beginning to end.</note> with the printed copy was clearly necessary."

\*) **Robert Holmes** used that one; for he certainly testifies in the place cited: "I extract the variants from Grabe's collation, yet I occasionally call upon the other one by Ussher for assistance." <note>original: „Ex Grabii collatione Variantes excerpo, alteram tamen Usserii in subsidium subinde devoco.“</note>

<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of the Cotton Genesis (Codex Cottonianus), transitioning from the history of its scholarship to its specific linguistic features, its estimated age, and a description of its famous illustrations.</meta>

<page-num>XXV</page-num>
<sig>d</sig>

Thus, it usually retains the "movable nu" <term>movable nu (ν ἐφελκυστικὸν): a letter 'n' added to the end of Greek words to avoid a vowel clash, though here it appears even before consonants</term> even before a consonant. Likewise, in the instances where Grabe's notes—written before the fire—are considered, it everywhere maintains the forms *we shall take* <note>original: ληψομεθα</note>, *let it be taken* <note>original: ληφθητω</note>, and *you may be taken along with* <note>original: συμπαραληφθης</note> (using *sym-* <note>συμπ.</note> rather than *syn-* <note>συνπ.</note>). It also has *four* <note>original: τεσσερα</note> and *Benjamin* <note>original: βενιαμειν</note>. Likewise it has *sword* <note>original: μαχαιρη</note>, *woman* <note>original: γυναικαν (11, 31. it was once readable; the passage is now lost)</note>, *in old age* <note>original: εν γηρει</note>, *ninety* <note>original: ενενηκοντα</note>, *cataracts* <note>original: καταρακται</note>, *turn back* <note>original: αποστραφητι</note>, *is it not...?* <note>original: ουχ ιδου (13, 9. in a place now lost)</note>, *it became* <note>original: εγεινετο</note>, *it was able* <note>original: εδυνκτο</note>, *terebinth tree* <note>original: τερεμινθον</note>, *drachmas* <note>original: δραχματα (instead of δραχμ.)</note>, and *those who curse* <note>original: καταρουμενους (12, 3. now lost)</note>. Nor are faults lacking, arising from the confusion of letters, especially vowels, such as *we might see* <note>original: ειδειμεν (rare cases of this kind)</note>, *of a child* instead of *of a plain* <note>original: παιδιου for πεδιου</note> and similar errors.

Having set these matters forth, we ask regarding the antiquity of the codex. In this question, there is no reason to refute those dreaming of the age of Origen; nor truly, with Owen and others, can it be called "perhaps the oldest of all Europe." For it is undoubtedly more recent than all those codices that shrink from the use of initial capital letters. Since those indeed seem to belong to the fourth century, nothing stands in the way of our deciding that the Cotton codex—along with several others with which it agrees most closely, except that the system of three-dot punctuation usually only takes hold in slightly later works—belongs to roughly the fifth century.

The truly singular ornament of the Cotton codex is found in the paintings, by which the events described in the text are represented. Many traces of these also survive to this day; some are still seen almost entirely intact, with colors clear enough although in poor condition. We have found it sufficient to briefly indicate in this edition the place where they were painted. Four specimens of them are exhibited in Owen’s little book at page XIII, taken from the two plates which were published in the year 1744 at the expense of the Society of Antiquaries of London under this title: "Certain fragments of a very ancient copy of the book of Genesis, adorned with elegant paintings."*) Likewise, the author of *Palaeographia Sacra Pictoria*, J. O. Westwood, recently provided a third plate in London, 1843–45.**) Furthermore, those paintings—which they say once numbered 250, distributed over 165 leaves—are as rare as they are highly praised for their antiquity; they excel with no common art and are preferred to similar paintings found in the purple Vienna Genesis codex.

---

*) There is a strange defectiveness in the writing of these specimens inserted in Owen’s book, proceeding from the ignorance of the editors, not from the codex itself. To say nothing of the shapes of the letters, from which the true nature of the codex can least be learned, we will give examples of the corrupted text itself. From Genesis 11:12 and following, there is shown: *-- n the Cainan -- the birth -- eight hundred: and e -- and daught.* These should be compared to page 101, XV. B., where it becomes clear not only that many more letters could be read by us, but also that instead of "eight hundred: and" <note>ακοσια· και</note>, "eight hundred thirty" <note>ακοσια τριακον</note> was transcribed according to the evidence of the codex: evidently "and e" <note>και ε</note> flowed from an error. In another place, Genesis 12:5 and following, it is inscribed in this manner: *-- Harran and: e -- into the land of Chanlan -- lln and aiwaeu -- as the place of sy -- n high: wa -- they inhabited the g --.* In these, the codex itself has *Chanaan* for *Chanlan*, *aan* for *lln*, *diodeu* for *aiwaeu*, and *oi de* for *wa*: compare page 103, XVIII. B. The two remaining passages transcribed in the plate are no longer found among the fragments in the British Museum. One, Genesis 43:13 and following, has: *and the brother* <note>original: λαελφον, corrected to αδελφ.</note> *of you -- standing go down* <note>original: καταβηγε, corrected to -τε</note> *to -- face. And may* <note>original: αε, corrected to δε</note> *my God give* <note>original: λωη, corrected to δωη</note> *-- mercy before the man. And your brother* <note>original: ααελφον, corrected to αδ-</note> *Benjamin. For I indeed as -- I have been childless.* The other, Genesis 43:30 and following, thus: *-- for his heart yearned -- for his brother* <note>original: ααελφω, corrected to αδ-</note>. *And he sought -- having entered* <note>original: εισελθων</note> *but* <note>original: αε, corrected to δε</note> *into the chamber -- there; and having washed his -- having come out* <note>original: ξελθων</note> *he restrained himself.* The form of the letters *alpha* (α) and *delta* (δ), everywhere confused in these examples, is by no means the same in the codex.

**) He rightly criticizes the specimens shown in the previous century. However, in transcribing some Greek words, he himself slipped. For the specimen he gave agrees with our text XXI. B. on page 105, except that in verse 11 he wrongly wrote *mambri* for *mamre* <note>original: προ μαμβρη written as μαμβρι</note>.
