Now, indeed, as we survey the individual fragments of the text with notes interspersed here and there, we shall indicate which additions of variant readings can be adopted from Owen’s collation of Grabe’s work.

**Page 95.** Grabe indicated the beginning of the Cotton manuscript with these words: <term>"evening, and there was morning, the third day:"</term> <note>original Greek: εσπερα, και εγενετο πρωι, ημερα τριτη</note> Genesis 1, 13. In verse 14, it is clear that the word <term>"upon"</term> <note>original Greek: επι</note> is missing. According to Grabe’s testimony, the word <term>"earth"</term> <note>original Greek: γης</note> is followed by: <term>"and let them rule over the day and over the night,"</term> <note>original Greek: και αρχετωσαν της ημερας και της νυκτος</note> of which we have published the remaining traces. As for Grabe’s note that "these were written by a more recent hand, though using ancient letters that seem to have been newly drawn," this should be qualified so that the addition itself must undoubtedly be attributed to the original scribe, even if a later corrector restored it. Likewise, the final <term>"into"</term> <note>original Greek: εις</note> is missing. In verse 18, <term>"he saw"</term> <note>original Greek: ιδεν</note> appears instead of <term>"he saw"</term> <note>original Greek: ειδεν</note>. From verse 20 until the end of verse 25, the text is missing. In verse 26, it is said that <term>"the"</term> <note>original Greek: των</note> is missing before <term>"creeping things,"</term> <note>original Greek: ερπετων</note> unless perhaps "the scribe, writing with a more recent hand, omitted 'the' <note>των</note> while adding 'and all' <note>και παν</note>." Moreover, all the portions we published under section II. A. were undoubtedly supplied by a second hand (roughly of the eighth century). The abbreviations <term>"of God"</term> <note>original Greek: θλσυ and θσυ, likely scribal variants for θεου</note> written on this page can be said to be almost entirely foreign to the first hand; although in 6, 16, according to Grabe’s testimony, the Cotton manuscript itself had <term>"cubit"</term> <note>original Greek: πηχην</note> instead of <term>"cubit"</term> <note>original Greek: πηχυν</note>. In the margin at verse 11 and following, something was written in minuscule letters, from which approximately the letters <term>ple - - te - - ba - -</term> <note>original Greek: πλη - - τε - - βα - -</note> remain.*) On the other page of this leaf, no writing survives, nor does anything seem to have been written. In verse 28, instead of <term>"fill,"</term> <note>original Greek: πληρωσατε</note> the Cotton manuscript is said to have <term>"multiply,"</term> <note>original Greek: πληθυν[ετε]</note> to which word the letter <term>th</term> <note>the Greek letter theta: θ</note> at the start of line 12 seems to refer. Regarding verse 31, Grabe says: "written plainly by a recent hand, it reads as in the Roman edition." Regarding Chapter II, Grabe notes: "This entire chapter is missing, except for the final words <term>'he shall be joined'</term> <note>original Greek: προσκολληθησεται</note> until the end, in which the Cotton manuscript agrees with the printed Roman edition."

**Page 96.** According to Grabe’s testimony, in the Cotton manuscript verse 1, <term>"more prudent"</term> <note>original Greek: φρονιμωτερος, comparative</note> appears instead of <term>"most prudent"</term> <note>original Greek: φρονιμωτατος, superlative</note>; in verse 5, <term>"he had known"</term> <note>original Greek: ειδει</note> instead of <term>"he knew"</term> <note>original Greek: ηδει</note>; <term>"that in the"</term> <note>original Greek: οτι εν η</note> instead of <term>"that the"</term> <note>original Greek: οτι η</note>; and <term>"your"</term> <note>original Greek: υμων</note> instead of <term>"our"</term> <note>original Greek: ημων</note>. However, it omits from verse 6 until the end of the chapter. Likewise, it omits from 4, 5 <term>"his sacrifices"</term> <note>original Greek: θυσιαις αυτου</note> until 4, 17 <term>"having conceived."</term> <note>original Greek: συλλαβουσα</note> In verse 17, <term>"of the son"</term> <note>original Greek: του υιου</note> is missing. In verse 18, we published <term>"Mehujael,"</term> <note>original Greek: μαουϊλ</note> and that reading must be maintained. Grabe has: <term>"Mehujael,"</term> <note>original Greek: μαουηλ</note> "although in the place of 'l' <note>λ</note>, an 'a' <note>α</note> was mistakenly written." But that the writer intended <term>-ia</term> <note>-ια</note> and not <term>-il</term> <note>-ιλ</note> is made probable even by the dots over the 'i' <note>ι</note>. Again, Grabe notes: "from those words in verse 18, <term>'the Lamech'</term> <note>original Greek: τον λαμεχ</note> until verse 25, everything is missing in the manuscript."

**Page 97.** According to Grabe’s testimony, in 5, 4, the Cotton manuscript omits <term>"which he lived"</term> <note>original Greek: ας εζησε</note> and has <term>"seven hundred years"</term> <note>original Greek: επτακ. ετη</note> instead of <term>"years, seven hundred"</term>; in 5, 8, after <term>"Seth,"</term> <note>original Greek: σηθ</note> it adds <term>"which he lived;"</term> likewise in verse 11, after <term>"nine hundred,"</term> it repeats <term>"years."</term> <note>original Greek: ετη</note> However, verse 12 and following until verse 18 are missing.

<term>clean</term> <note>original: "καθαρων"</note> until <term>female</term>; <note>original: "θηλυ"</note> in verse 3, he first added <term>all</term> <note>original: "παντων"</note> before <term>birds</term>, <note>original: "πετεινων"</note> but omitted it before the second instance; in verse 4, he had <term>forty</term> <note>original: "τεσσερακοντα"</note> twice, likewise <term>all the substance</term> <note>original: "πασαν την αναστασιν"</note> (compare X. An addition to <term>ark</term> <note>original: "κιβωτον"</note> in verse 13, the remains of which we have published, according to Grabe's witness has it thus: <term>because of the water of the flood</term>. <note>original: "δια το υδωρ του κατακλυσμου"</note> In verse 14, the same scholar testifies that <term>bird</term> <note>original: "ορνεον"</note> and <term>its</term> <note>original: "αυτου"</note> were omitted; in verse 15, <term>male and female</term> <note>original: "αρσεν και θηλυ"</note> were omitted; in verse 16, <term>they entered</term> <note>original: "εισηλθον"</note> instead of <term>he entered</term> <note>original: "-λθε"</note> and <term>Lord</term> <note>original: "κυριος"</note> (a word we ourselves have published) instead of <term>the God</term> <note>original: "ο θεος"</note>; furthermore, the words from <term>and he shut</term> <note>original: "και εκλεισε"</note> in verse 16 until <term>and it was lifted up</term> <note>original: "και υψωθη"</note> in verse 24 were passed over; in verse 24, <term>fifty and a hundred days</term> <note>original: "πεντηκ. και εκατ. ημερας"</note> instead of <term>a hundred and fifty days</term>. <note>original: "ημερ. εκατ. πεντηκ."</note>

**Pag. 100.** Grabe witnesses that in 8, 1, <term>he remembered</term> <note>original: "εμνησθη"</note> was used instead of <term>he called to mind</term> <note>original: "ανεμνησθη"</note> and <term>and of all the creeping things that creep</term> <note>original: "και παντων τ. ερπετων των ερποντων"</note> was omitted; in verse 3, <term>it abated</term> <note>original: "ενεδιδου"</note> (see our note at XII. B. verse 7) instead of <term>all the face of the earth</term>, <note>original: "παν το προσωπον της γης"</note> and <term>him</term> <note>original: "αυτον"</note> ("written by a more recent hand, but perhaps following the traces of ancient letters") instead of <term>himself</term>. <note>original: "εαυτον"</note> As for the witness that <term>his</term> <note>original: "αυτου"</note> was added after <term>hand</term> in the same verse (following the style of the Alexandrian Codex), it appears that this may have resulted from an error regarding a punctuation mark in line 8 of our edition; however, by calculating the individual letters of the following verse, it becomes quite probable that <term>his</term> <note>original: "αυτου"</note> was written at the beginning of verse 9.

In verse 11, according to Grabe's witness, <term>the</term> <note>original: "το"</note> is missing before <term>toward</term>, and <term>face of</term> <note>original: "προσωπου"</note> is added before <term>the earth</term>; in verse 13, <term>one of the month</term> <note>original: "μια του μηνος"</note> is omitted, and instead of <term>he saw that it failed</term>, <note>original: "ειδεν οτι εξελιπε"</note> there was written <term>he saw that it had subsided</term>; <note>original: "ιδεν οτι κεκοπακεν"</note> in verse 14, there was read <term>in the second month on the twenty-seventh day of the month the earth was dried</term> <note>original: "εν δε τω μηνι τω δευτερω εβδομη και εικαδι του μηνος εξηρανθη η γη"</note> instead of the text from <term>in the</term> until <term>twenty-seventh of the month</term>; in verse 15, <term>to Noah</term> <note>original: "τω νωε"</note> instead of <term>toward Noah</term>; <note>original: "προς νωε"</note> in verse 19, <term>creeping thing and every bird and everything moving</term> <note>original: "ερπετον και παν πετεινον και παν κινουμενον"</note> instead of <term>bird and every creeping thing moving</term>; <note>original: "πετεινον και παν ερπετον κινουμενον"</note> in the same place, <term>they went out</term> <note>original: "εξηλθον"</note> instead of <term>they went out</term>; <note>original: "εξηλθοσαν" - a grammatical variant</note> verses 20 and 21 until <term>then yet to strike</term> <note>original: "ουν ετι παπαξαι"</note> were omitted.

**Pag. 101.** Grabe witnesses that in 9, 1, the codex has <term>he blessed</term>; <note>original: "ηυλογησεν"</note> in verse 2, <term>your dread and the fear</term> <note>original: "τρομος υμων κ. ο φοβ."</note> instead of <term>the dread and your fear</term>; <meta>This page continues the catalog of textual variations found in the Cotton Genesis fragments, moving through Genesis chapters 10 to 18. It records the findings of scholar Johannes Ernst Grabe (1666–1711), who examined the manuscript before it was heavily damaged by fire.</meta>

<term>beginning</term> <note>original: "αρχη"</note>, <term>Orech</term> <note>original: "ορεχ"</note> instead of <term>and Orech</term> <note>original: "και ορεχ"</note>, <term>Achad</term> <note>original: "αχαδ"</note> instead of <term>Archad</term> <note>original: "αρχαδ"</note>, (he says <term>Chalaunē</term> <note>original: "χαλαυνη"</note> was written by a more recent hand), <term>earth</term> <note>original: "γη"</note> instead of <term>the earth</term> <note>original: "τη γη"</note>, <term>Sennaar</term> <note>original: "σεννααρ"</note>. Verse 11: <term>Nineveh</term> <note>original: "νινευη"</note> (Grabe adds "superscripted"), <term>Rooboth</term> <note>original: "ροωβοθ"</note> (he adds "written by a more recent hand"), <term>Chalach</term> <note>original: "χαλαχ"</note> instead of <term>the Chalach</term> <note>original: "την χαλαχ"</note>. Verse 12: <term>Dasem</term> <note>original: "δασεμ"</note> instead of <term>Dasē</term> <note>original: "δαση"</note> and <term>the</term> <note>original: "η"</note> ("by a more recent hand, but perhaps from ancient traces") <term>great</term> <note>original: "μεγαλη"</note>. Having omitted from verse 13 until chapter 11, verse 4, he proceeds from 11:4 with <term>that we be scattered</term> <note>original: "σπαρηναι ημας"</note>; verse 6: <term>of them</term> <note>original: "εξ αυτων"</note>; verse 7: <term>has their language</term> <note>original: "εχει τ. γλ. αυτων"</note> and <term>his neighbor</term> <note>original: "πλησιον αυτου"</note>; verse 9: twice <term>the Lord God</term> <note>original: "κυριος ο θεος"</note>; verse 10: <term>and he was</term> <note>original: "και ην"</note> omitted; verse 11: <term>five hundred years</term> <note>original: "πεντακοσια ετη"</note>; verse 12: <term>years</term> <note>original: "ετη"</note> (see under XV. B. verse 1) <term>one hundred thirty</term> <note>original: "εκατ. τριακ."</note>. 

**Page 102.** These items are noteworthy: verse 13: <term>one hundred thirty years</term> <note>original: "ετη εκατ. τρ."</note> instead of <term>one hundred and thirty years</term>; verse 14: <term>one hundred thirty years</term>; verse 15: <term>thirty thirty years</term> <note>original: "ετη τριακ. τριακοντ." - likely a scribal duplication error in the manuscript</note>; verse 16: <term>one hundred thirty years</term>, likewise <term>forty</term> <note>original: "τεσσερακ." - a spelling variant</note> instead of <term>forty</term> <note>original: "τεσσαρ."</note>; verse 16: <term>Phalek</term> <note>original: "φαλεκ"</note> (compare under XVI. B. verse 4). Indeed, from verse 28 to verse 29, everything was already missing at that time. At verse 29, he noted <term>he took</term> <note>original: "ελαβεν"</note> instead of <term>they took</term> <note>original: "ελαβον"</note>; verse 31: <term>and Nahor</term> <note>original: "και τον ναχωρ"</note> added to <term>Abram</term>, in the same place <term>wife</term> <note>original: "γυναικαν"</note>, <term>to go</term> <note>original: "πορευεσθαι"</note>, <term>he came</term> <note>original: "ηλθεν"</note>; verse 32: <term>all</term> <note>original: "πασαι"</note> and <term>earth</term> <note>original: "γη"</note> omitted, likewise <term>two hundred five years</term> <note>original: "ετη διαχ. πεντε"</note>. 

**Page 103.** Through the whole of chapter 12, Grabe testifies to these: verse 1: <term>and come hither</term> <note>original: "και δευρο"</note> are missing; verse 2: is <term>blessed</term> <note>original: "ευλογητος"</note>; verse 3: is <term>those who curse</term> <note>original: "καταρουμενους"</note>; verse 4: <term>with the</term> <note>original: "μετα του"</note> instead of <term>with him</term>; verse 5: <term>the Sarah</term> <note>original: "την σαραν"</note> instead of <term>Sarah the</term>, <term>he went out</term> <note>original: "εξηλθεν"</note> instead of <term>they went out</term>, after <term>Canaan</term> was added <term>and he came into the land of Canaan</term> <note>original: "και ηλθεν εις γην χανααν"</note>; verse 6: <term>into the length of it</term> <note>original: "εις το μηκος αυτης"</note> are missing; verse 8: <term>in</term> <note>original: "εν"</note> is omitted; verse 11: after <term>wife</term> is added <term>his</term> <note>original: "αυτου" — Grabe notes this was "written by a more recent hand as if a catchword"</note>; verse 12 through 13:7: <term>but the Canaanites</term> <note>original: "οι δε χαναναιοι"</note> are missing. 

**Page 104.** We have indicated that the fragments under XX. A. and B. were disrupted. Grabe notes the following for chapter 13: verse 8: <term>and you</term> <note>original: "και σου"</note> are missing, in the same place <term>we are</term> <note>original: "ημεις εσμεν"</note> instead of <term>are we</term>, <term>is it not behold</term> <note>original: "ουχ ιδου"</note>; verse 10: <term>his</term> <note>original: "αυτου"</note> is missing; verse 10: <term>he saw</term> <note>original: "ιδεν"</note> instead of <term>he beheld</term>; verse 12: <term>but</term> <note>original: "δε"</note> after <term>Abram</term> is missing, but superscripted by a more recent hand; verse 14: <term>to be separated</term> <note>original: "χωρισθηναι"</note> instead of <term>to be divided</term>, <term>having looked up</term> <note>original: "αναβλεψας"</note> instead of <term>look up</term>, and <term>and</term> before <term>behold</term> is missing, <term>north</term> <note>original: "βορρα" — a spelling variant</note> instead of <term>north</term> <note>original: "βορραν"</note>; verse 15: <term>all the land</term> <note>original: "πασα η γη"</note> instead of <term>all the land</term> <note>original: "πασαν την γην" — a grammatical case change</note>; verse 15: <term>of the age</term> <note>original: "του αιωνος"</note>; verse 17: to <term>breadth</term> is added <term>of it</term> <note>original: "αυτης"</note>, the words <term>and to your seed forever</term> are missing; verse 18: <term>Hebron</term>. Many of these are also recognized from our fragments. For chapter 14, Grabe notes: verse 1: <term>and</term> is missing before <term>Arioch</term>, <term>king</term> <note>original: "βασιλευς"</note> instead of <term>of the king</term>, <term>and Chedorlaomer</term>, <term>Sailam</term> instead of <term>Elam</term>, <term>Thalgal</term> or <term>Thalgad</term> instead of <term>Thargal</term>; verse 2: <term>with</term> <note>original: "μετα"</note> is missing before <term>Sennaar</term> and <term>Bymobor</term>; verse 4: <term>they</term> <note>original: "αυτοι"</note> omitted, <term>they were serving</term> <note>original: "εδουλευον"</note> instead of <term>they served</term>, <term>Chedorlaomer</term>. <summary>This page lists specific textual variants in Genesis chapters 10 through 18 as observed by Johannes Ernst Grabe in the Cotton Genesis manuscript. It compares unusual word choices, omissions, and additions—such as the number of years for patriarchs or the spelling of names—against the standard Greek biblical text.</summary>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the detailed collation of textual variants from the Cotton Genesis manuscript (an illuminated Greek manuscript of the 5th or 6th century), focusing on Genesis chapters 18 through 22. It follows the scholarly tradition of comparing different codices and printed editions to reconstruct the most accurate biblical text.</meta>

<term>let it be taken</term> <note>original: "ληφθητω"</note>, verse 5. <term>I will take</term> <note>original: "ληψομαι"</note>, likewise (as also appears from our edition under section 25. A. verse 7 and following) the phrases <term>on your way</term> <note>original: "εις την οδον υμων"</note>, <term>for the sake of</term> <note>original: "εστ εινεκεν"</note>, and <term>they said so</term> <note>original: "ειπαν ουτως"</note> are missing; likewise <term>according to</term> <note>original: "καθα"</note> instead of <term>just as</term> <note>original: "καθώς"</note>. <vocab>Patrick Young, Johannes Ernst Grabe, apparatus criticus, textual variants, Genesis, Greek manuscripts, missing, instead of</vocab>

<summary>This page records technical textual variations in Genesis chapters 18 to 22, comparing the Cotton Genesis manuscript against other versions. It highlights specific scribal errors, corrections by later hands, and damage (such as carbonized pages) that affected the legibility of the ancient Greek text.</summary>
<keywords>Cotton Genesis, Johannes Ernst Grabe, textual criticism, Septuagint variants, biblical manuscripts, Genesis 18-22, scribal corrections</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical collation of textual variants from the Cotton Genesis manuscript, specifically examining small discrepancies in spelling, missing words, and alternate readings in Genesis chapters 22 through 25. It compares the manuscript's text with standard editions and notes where previous scholars, like John Ernest Grabe, observed corrections or physical damage to the parchment.</meta>

**Regarding Chapter 30:**

<meta>This page continues the detailed collation of the Cotton Genesis manuscript, specifically documenting textual variations in the Greek text of Genesis from chapters 40 through 45. <page-num>XXXIV</page-num>

"I thought," <note>original: "ωμην"</note> verse 17. In the phrase "in the basket," <note>original: "εν δε τω κανω"</note> the words "of the kinds" <note>original: "των γενων"</note> are missing. Verse 18: it reads "the king Pharko," <note>original: "ο βασιλευς φαρκω"</note> while "of the heaven" <note>original: "του ουρανου"</note> is missing. Verse 19: "you will take away" <note>original: "αφελειτε"</note>. Verse 20: "of the chief cupbearer" and "of the chief baker" <note>original: "αρχιοινοχοου et αρχισιτοποιου"</note>. (**Page 158.**) Verse 23: "but he did not remember" <note>original: "ουκ εμνησθη δε"</note>.

**Regarding Chapter 41:** Verse 4: "the words 'in the flesh' <note>original: "ταις σαρξι"</note> are missing" (though one should write it with the terminal 'n' as *sarxin* <note>original: "-ξιν"</note>, for in the earlier instance where it is written *sarxi* <note>original: "-ξι"</note>, they are not missing. Holmes <note>original: "Holmesius"</note> is mistaken.). "But they appear at the bottom of the page." For my part, I saw nothing written there: compare LXXXII. B. 16. (**Page 159.** Nothing new.), Verse 13. (**Page 160.**) "thus" <note>original: "ουτως"</note>. Verse 15. (**Page 161.**) "to Joseph" <note>original: "τω ιωσηφ"</note> (in the same place he notes "he saw" <note>original: "εορα"</note>, but it should be corrected from our published editions to "he has seen" <note>original: "εορακκ"</note>). Verse 19: "land of Egypt" <note>original: "γη αιγυπτω"</note> (compare LXXXV. A. 18.). (**Page 162.**) Verse 24: "it" <note>original: "αυτο"</note> is missing (in the same verse "full" <note>original: "πληρης"</note> appears instead of "full [plural]" <note>original: "πληρεις"</note>; compare LXXXV. B. 6., which was not noticed by Grabe). Verse 26: "there is in" <note>original: "εν ενεστι"</note>. Verse 30. (**Page 163.**) That the phrase "of the coming [famine]" <note>original: "της εσομενης"</note> is missing is sufficiently known from the first verse under LXXXVI. A.; likewise, it is written "in all the earth" <note>original: "εν ολη γη"</note>. Verse 37: "words" <note>original: "ρηματα"</note> instead of "the word" <note>original: "το ρημα"</note>, as we published in the place cited, line 24; however, the word "the" <note>original: "τα"</note> was noted above the line in smaller but elegant uncial <note>a script used in ancient manuscripts consisting entirely of capital letters</note> letters, apparently by the original hand, which Grabe did not notice. After "Joseph" <note>original: "ιωσηφ"</note> in verse 41, everything is missing until verse 45. In verse 49: "as" <note>original: "ως"</note> instead of "as if" <note>original: "ωσει"</note> and "to number" <note>original: "αριθμησαι"</note>, as we published in LXXXVII. B. 1 and 4. Verse 50 with those following is missing until verse 55. In verse 56: "upon the face" <note>original: "επι προσωπον"</note>, as can be seen on page 165, LXXXVIII. A. 1.

**Regarding Chapter 42:** Verse 1: "you are idle" <note>original: "ραθυμειται"</note>. Verse 7: "they said" <note>original: "ειπαν"</note>. In verse 15, he testifies with us (compare **page 166**, verse 20) to the reading "by the [life of]" <note>original: "μα την"</note>, but in verse 16, where the text has now perished, he adds that it was "yes, by the [life of]" <note>original: "νη την"</note>. In verse 19, he notes that "into" <note>original: "εις"</note> is placed before "let him be held back" <note>original: "κατασχεθητω"</note>; yet I cannot but believe this arose from an error: compare **page 167**, LXXXX. B. 4. In verse 21, "and" <note>original: "και"</note> is missing before "on account of" <note>original: "ενεκεν"</note> (as we published in LXXXI. A. verse 10). Verse 24 and those following are missing until the words "saying to one another" <note>original: "αλληλους λεγοντες"</note> in verse 28. In verse 28: "God has done" <note>original: "ο θεος εποιησεν"</note>. Verse 29: "in the land" <note>original: "εν γη"</note> instead of "into the land" <note>original: "εις γην"</note> (Owen <note>original: "Owenius"</note> notes: "these words, 'into the land' <note>original: "εις γην"</note>, are crossed out with a pen in the manuscript. Whether the words 'in the land' <note>original: "εν γη"</note> should also be crossed out, I do not know."). Verse 32: "younger" <note>original: "μικροτερος"</note>. Verse 33: "one of your brothers" <note>original: "αδελφον υμων ενα"</note>. Verse 35: "them" <note>original: "αυτους"</note> is missing. (**Page 168.**) Verse 36: "Benjamin" <note>original: "βενιαμειν"</note>. ### Regarding Chapter 47:

**Verse 1.** "he reported," the words "and all their things" are missing. **Verse 4.** "we shall dwell, your servants" <note>original: "κατοικησομεν οι παιδες σου"</note> and "you know" <note>original: "επιστασε"</note>. **Verse 5.** "to Joseph" (in the same place it is noted: "N.B. <note>Note Well</note> 'they have come' <note>original: "ηκασι"</note> without the letter 'n' at the end."). **Verse 7.** "he blessed." **Verse 9.** "they arrived" <note>original: "αφεικοντο"</note>, then instead of "of the life" <note>original: "της ζωης"</note> it is noted: "'of the years' <note>original: "των ετων"</note> are repeated," likewise instead of "days" <note>original: "ημερας"</note> there is: "days which" <note>original: "ημεραι ας"</note>. In both places, an error seems to be present. **Verse 11.** the words "in the land of Rameses" are missing, "Pharaoh commanded him." **Verse 12.** the first "his" <note>original: "αυτου"</note> is missing, then it is "to his brothers." **Verse 13.** "it failed" <note>original: "εξελειπεν"</note>, likewise in **Verse 15.** **Verse 14.** "in Egypt." **Verse 15.** the word "our" <note>original: "ημων"</note> is missing. **Verse 16.** the last "your" <note>original: "υμων"</note> is missing. **Verse 17.** the first "their" <note>original: "αυτων"</note> is missing. **Verse 18.** the second "our" <note>original: "ημων"</note> is missing. **Verse 19.** "it shall be made desolate," the word "the" <note>original: "τω"</note> is missing before "Pharaoh," "seeds," "that we may live." **Verse 20.** "the land of Pharaoh," **Verse 22.** the word "this" <note>original: "ταυτην"</note> is missing. From verse 20 until the end of the chapter, everything is missing.

**Verse 1.** "it was reported," "his" instead of "your," "his sons with himself." **Verse 3.** the word "my" <note>original: "μου"</note> is missing. **Verse 5.** the word "land" <note>original: "γη"</note> is missing, it reads "as Simeon." **Verse 6.** "if" <note>original: "αν"</note> instead of "if" <note>original: "εαν"</note>, "they shall be to you," "in" <note>original: "εν"</note> instead of "upon" <note>original: "επι"</note> at the end. **Verse 7.** the word "the" <note>original: "της"</note> is missing before "Syria," "Rachel" is placed after "your mother," "Chabratha." **Verse 8.** "who are these" <note>original: "τινες ησιν" — written as 'hysin'</note> instead of "who [are] these to you." **Verse 10.** "but his eyes" and "they were able." **Verse 12.** "upon the earth." **Verse 13.** "Manasseh" and "with the left" <note>original: "τη αριστερα"</note> instead of "from the left" in the second instance. **Verse 14.** "the right hand" and "having crossed [his hands]" <note>original: "εναλλαξας"</note>. **Verse 15.** "before him," "of my youth." **Verse 17.** the words "the hand" are missing. **Verse 19.** "he shall be." **Verse 21.** "out of this land into the." **Verse 22.** "I give" <note>original: "δωμι" — noted as such</note> instead of "I give" <note>original: "διδωμι"</note> and "with a sword."

### Regarding Chapter 49:

**Verse 1.** the word "to them" is missing, it is "I may announce." **Verse 2.** the words "listen Israel, listen" are missing. **Verse 5.** "Leuei" <note>Levi</note>, in the same place "from a division" <note>original: "εξ αιρεσεως"</note> with a certain space cast between. **Verse 6.** the reading "to strive" <note>original: "ερισαι"</note> is confirmed, likewise **Verse 8.** "to you." **Verse 9.** "Judas" <note>Judah</note>. **Verse 10.** "it shall fail" and "if" <note>original: "αν"</note> instead of "if" <note>original: "εαν"</note>. **Verse 11.** "winding/vine-tendril." **Verse 12.** "from wine," in the same place the second "his" is missing. **Verse 13.** "he shall dwell." **Verse 16.** "his own people." **Verse 17.** "into the backward parts." **Verse 19.** "their pirate/raider." **Verse 20.** "he distributes food." **Verse 21.** "Nephthali" and "in the offspring." **Verse 22.** the first "my" is missing. **Verse 24.** "of their hands through the hand" (compare CIV. B. 5.). **Verse 26.** ("lo")gei <note>likely a fragment of a word</note> in the same place at verse 12 refers to "blessings of the father," therefore the manuscript protects "blessings" <note>original: "ευλογειας"</note>) "upon blessings," the words "and upon the blessings of the hills" are missing, "eternal" <note>original: "αιωνιων"</note> instead of "ever-flowing/everlasting" <note>original: "αενναων"</note>, "upon the head." **Verse 27.** "Beniamien" <note>Benjamin</note> and "he shall distribute." **Verse 29.** "bury!" **Verse 30.** "in the land of the Canaanites." **Verse 31.** "his" and "he has" twice. **Verse 33.** "Jacob having lifted up," "his" is missing, it is "he failed/ceased."

### Regarding Chapter 50.

From **Verse 2.** "the Israel" until **Verse 6.** everything is missing. **Verse 7.** the word "the" <note>original: "της"</note> is missing ("the letter gamma is clearly read, not tau"). **Verse 8.** "and the kinship" without "his," "they were left behind." **Verse 9.** the word "and" is missing before "chariots." **Verse 10.** "upon the threshing floor," "Atat," "the mourning" is placed after "his father." **Verse 11.** "in the threshing floor," "this is," "they called the name of the place." **Verse 12.** "his sons as he commanded them." **Verse 13.** "into the land." **Verse 14** and following until **Verse 21** are missing. **Verse 23.** "he saw," in the same place...

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page concludes the analysis of the Cotton Genesis (Codex Cottonianus) fragments and introduces a new manuscript acquisition. It focuses on the difficulty of reading heavily damaged fragments and provides a scholarly critique of previous editors like Grabe and Junius.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
XXXVI

in the words "and the sons" <note>original: "και οι υιοι"</note> it is noted: "the 'and' <note>original: 'και'</note> was added by a later hand and 'the' <note>original: 'οι'</note> was omitted;" in verse 24 [it reads] "into the land, and Isaac" <note>original: "εις γην, και ισαακ"</note>. Verse 26 [is] "written in a later hand in this manner: 'And Joseph died at one hundred and ten years old; and they buried him in a coffin in Egypt.' It seems, however, to have been transcribed from the final leaf, which has now perished."*)

Page 174. 175. 176. The earlier fragments of the Cottonian codex <note>A famous 5th-century Greek manuscript of Genesis, heavily damaged by fire in 1731</note> from page 95 to page 173 which we have presented—where they belong has for the most part already been indicated by a learned hand in the margins of those paper leaves in which the ancient remains were enclosed (as explained above), though occasionally not with enough accuracy. However, the tiny fragments <note>original: "fragmenta minuta"</note> that follow on pages 174, 175, 176, thirty-one in number, are more difficult and desperate than the rest, and contain no such notes. Nevertheless, it happened that we discovered where the nine that we published on page 174 belonged. Nor do I doubt that by long-term new studies most of the rest can be defined, just as we found three while publishing: CXXV. CXXXXII. A. and B. The first on page 175 belongs to Genesis 21:28 and 29. In line 1, read "seven ewe lambs" <note>original: "ε[π]τ[α] αμναδας"</note>. The third ends, it seems, "[Abra]ham: [what is it?]" <note>original: "[αβρ]ααμ· τ[ι εστιν]"</note>. In the fourth, read "[fo]rth sheep" <note>original: "[προ]βατων"</note> [with the number] 26 <note>this is missing the words "of these" found in the Roman edition</note>. In the sixth, the letters "anh" <note>original: "ανη"</note> are hardly correct. 

Indeed, through these remains—in which a whole word is rarely found and most parts are quite darkened—the shapes of the letters themselves must be examined more than the letters we have published: for much relies on conjecture. Hence **K** and **B**, **N** and **H** and **Π** <note>Pi</note>, **A** and **Δ** <note>Delta</note>, **Є** <note>Epsilon</note> and **Θ** <note>Theta</note> and **C** <note>Sigma</note>, **Θ** <note>Theta</note> and **O**, and those which have a similar appearance, could very easily be confused with one another <note>This refers to the difficulty of reading Greek Uncial script where letters often look alike when faded or burned</note>. The second fragment, CXXXXII. A. on page 176, is taken from Genesis 25:16 and following. In line 3, it seems "[Th]ese [are]" <note>original: "[Ο]υτο[ι εισιν]"</note> was written; line 4 is "[their] names" <note>original: "ονοματα [αυτων]"</note>; line 5 "[their and in]" <note>original: "αυτ[ων και εν]"</note>; line 6 "twelve" <note>original: "[δ]ωδεκα"</note>; line 7 "th[ese years]" <note>original: "τα[υτα τα ετη]"</note>; line 8 (following the letter 'e' in line 7) "one hundred" <note>original: "[ε]Κατον"</note>; line 9 is "[dy]ing [after those things before]" <note>original: "εκλει]πων"</note>; line 10 "to [the people]" <note>original: "προς τ[ο γενος"</note>. The final part: "paten" <note>original: "πατεν"</note> seems poorly edited. The third, CXXXXII. B., belongs to Genesis 25:23 and following, but the individual letters seem uncertain, except that in line 10 "ruddy" <note>original: "[πυ]ρραχη[ς]"</note> is beyond doubt.

---

### III.

# CODEX OF GENESIS FROM THE BODLEIAN LIBRARY RECENTLY BROUGHT FROM THE EAST.

That book is one of those which I happened to draw out from long-standing darkness in the year 1853, when I was traveling through eastern lands. I brought the most fortunately discovered treasure to Europe, and, after partially my own—

> *) At the end of the collation <note>The process of comparing different versions of a text</note> these things are written: "[On the last manuscript page, these questions are proposed by Grabe.] <note>John Ernest Grabe, a scholar known for his edition of the Septuagint</note> Whether Genesis 14:17 [is] 'of the kings' <note>original: 'βασιλεων'</note> or 'of the king' <note>original: 'βασιλεως'</note>. Whether in Genesis 19:15 'and he went out' <note>original: 'και εξηλθε'</note> (correct to 'go out' <note>original: 'εξελθε'</note>) is missing, as Junius <note>Franciscus Junius, a pioneer of Germanic philology and biblical scholar</note> says in this place. [In] 24:39 'should she not wish to go' <note>original: 'μη βουληται πορευθηναι'</note>, according to Junius. [In] 27:1 'he said: behold, I' <note>original: 'ειπεν ιδου εγω'</note>, or 'he said to him: behold, I' <note>original: 'ειπεν αυτω ιδου εγω'</note>. Whether Genesis 27:46 [is] 'I was weary of living' <note>original: 'προσωχθισα ζην'</note>, as Gale <note>Thomas Gale, an English classical scholar</note> noted." Most of these seem to have little credibility; as for what Junius noted regarding 19:15, we have shown above that it is clearly false. Furthermore, throughout this entire index of variant readings, we have not aimed to accurately note our own along with those of Grabe. We have only given samples of readings overlooked by Grabe here and there, just as we have also indicated several times that he has erred.

The page also introduces a new Genesis manuscript the author discovered in the East in 1853, now held at the Bodleian Library.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the preface of the volume. Having finished the description of the Cotton Genesis fragments (Codex Cottonianus) on the previous page, the author now transitions to describing the acquisition and physical characteristics of the Codex Bodleianus, a significant Greek manuscript of the book of Genesis now held at Oxford.</meta>

<page-type>preface</page-type>

### XXXVII

I had partly transcribed [the manuscript] with my own hand and partly arranged for it to be transcribed by a skilled hand. I then yielded it to the most distinguished curators of the **Bodleian Library**, which flourishes at **Oxford** <note>The main research library of the University of Oxford, one of the oldest in Europe</note> and is indeed most celebrated throughout the entire literary world for its remarkable abundance of Greek codices <note>A codex is an ancient manuscript in book form, rather than a scroll</note>. And so, a year and some time ago, my codex became a Bodleian one.

The parchment is quite sturdy, consisting of 29 leaves <note>original: "folia"</note> of the largest format. Each page is written in two columns. The number of lines written in individual columns varies between forty-four and thirty-seven. On the earlier pages, the writing is more compressed than on the later pages; hence, from the beginning of our edition, the lines tend to be longer than they are later on. From the specimen of the handwriting which we have provided on the stone-engraved plate numbered 2, it can be understood that the <term>uncial letters</term> <note>A style of script written entirely in large, rounded capital letters, commonly used in Greek and Latin manuscripts from the 4th to 8th centuries</note> in which it is written have a somewhat poorer appearance, or are of that kind which was most used in the eighth and ninth centuries, characterized by oblong and slanted forms. It is also distinguished by the now frequent use of accents and breathing marks <note>original: "accentuum spirituumque"; marks used in Greek to indicate pitch and vowel aspiration</note>, as well as complex punctuation. Consequently, the codex itself cannot be earlier than the eighth century, and I suspect it was written toward the end rather than the beginning of that century. This is of such a nature that I would have hardly judged it worthy of this collection of most ancient monuments, were it not that its great excellence of text compensated for its lesser claim to antiquity. Indeed, it seems that it should not be considered inferior to the **Codex Alexandrinus** <note>A famous 5th-century manuscript of the Greek Bible</note> or almost any other of the most ancient witnesses to the book of **Genesis**. Furthermore, the dignity of the Bodleian codex is increased by the fact that so few uncial codices of Genesis have survived to our times: for besides the Bodleian, no more than seven have become known. Of these, only two—one being the Alexandrinus in London, the other the **Coislinianus** in Paris—contain more of the text of Genesis than the Bodleian, while the remaining five consist of fewer fragments.

Just as we have arranged for the Bodleian codex to be transcribed in our uncial types—even though those types were adapted for older examples, a matter we already touched upon above on page 12—so too have we departed from perfect imitation in that we have not expressed the accents and similar marks. To compensate for this to some extent, we have added examples below from which the system of the codex in these matters will be sufficiently clear. Finally, in our edition, we also had to abandon the imitation of the two columns per page, as they were generally of a greater width than could be contained within the format of our book. However, having made the necessary adjustments to this format, we have accurately rendered individual lines and individual columns. For we have indicated the individual pages of the codex with the numbers I through LVIII, in such a way that the two columns of each page are distinguished by the letters A and B. Nevertheless, these matters are so arranged that—unless one prefers to gratefully acknowledge such a great treasure of sacred criticism, discovered by me, brought to Europe, and now, barely three years later, transcribed in type and shared with the learned world—I may have given diligent critics ample material for finding fault and teaching me better. But so that I do not begrudge them that, I will add those things which may serve to illustrate the codex and our edition, and I will speak briefly concerning:

1.  The excellence of the text.
2.  The defects of the writing.
3.  The corrections visible in the codex.
4.  The accents and similar marks.
5.  The punctuation.
6.  The abbreviations <note>original: "compendiis"</note>.

And first, indeed, intending to speak of the excellence of the text, I shall repeat an example which I have already used in my *Sacred and Profane Anecdotes* <note>original: "Anecdotis meis sacris et profanis"; referring to Tischendorf's 1855 publication</note>: [Genesis] 31:31–47. There, 45 readings differ from the Roman edition—for which, throughout the first 46 chapters, the most famous **Vatican Codex** <note>Codex Vaticanus, a 4th-century manuscript</note> could not be used. In 13 places, it agrees with the Codex Alexandrinus; but in 9 others—with the **Cotton Codex** (the remains of which we published above) agreeing six times (it is missing in three)—it stands against...

<summary>The author describes the physical characteristics and scholarly value of the Codex Bodleianus, a Greek Genesis manuscript he donated to Oxford. He argues that despite its later date (8th-9th century), its textual quality rivals the most ancient manuscripts like the Codex Alexandrinus.</summary>

<keywords>Codex Bodleianus, Oxford, Bodleian Library, Genesis, uncial script, manuscript description, Codex Alexandrinus, textual criticism, Tischendorf</keywords>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of the Bodleian manuscript of Genesis. The author is comparing its text to other famous versions like the Codex Alexandrinus and the Codex Cottonianus to determine its lineage and accuracy.</meta>

<page-num>XXXVIII</page-num>

It follows the Alexandrian reading. Furthermore, it often agrees with the **Cotton manuscript** <note>Codex Cottonianus, a 4th or 5th-century Greek manuscript of Genesis</note> even when the **Alexandrian codex** and the **Roman edition** <note>The 1587 Sixtine edition of the Septuagint</note> disagree, such as in verse 32, where the words "and he said... of us" <note>original: "καὶ εἶπεν - - ἡμῶν"</note> etc., are transposed in both, and in verse 40 where both support the readings "I was" <note>original: "ἐγινώμην"</note> and "frost" <note>original: "παγετός"</note>. In verse 33, along with the **Vienna Purple Codex**, it omits the word "into" <note>original: "εἰς"</note> against the Roman edition and the Alexandrian codex. Notable also is the agreement between the **Bodleian** and **Venetian** codices, regarding which **Holmes** <note>Robert Holmes, an 18th-century scholar who led a massive project to collate Septuagint manuscripts</note>, who catalogs it as number 72, judges thus:

> “It is indeed a unique codex of its kind and to be highly valued; for it contains a text hitherto unknown.”

With this codex alone, the Bodleian omits the words from "answering" to "Laban" <note>original: "ἀποκριθεὶς... Λάβαν"</note> in verse 36; likewise, in verse 37, it alone maintains the corrupt reading "I will place" <note>original: "θέσω δὲ"</note> instead of "place here" <note>original: "θὲς ὧδε"</note> (see later where accents are discussed). Since it provides several other readings almost exclusively, such as in verses 45, 47, and 42 where it has "for a pillar," "and he called," and "yesterday" <note>original: "εἰς στήλην, ἐκάλεσεν δέ, ἐχθές"</note>; in verses 37 and 46 where it omits "that" and "there" <note>original: "ὅτι" and "ἐκεῖ"</note>; and in many places has entirely unique readings, such as in verse 33 "he entered into" <note>original: "εἰσῆλθεν εἰς"</note>; in verse 37 "you have searched" <note>original: "ἠραύνηκας"</note> (the rest "you searched" <note>original: "ἠρεύνησας"</note>, three others "you have searched" <note>original: "ἠρεύνηκας"</note>); in the same place "you have found" <note>original: "εὔρηκας"</note> (the rest "you found" <note>original: "εὗρες"</note>); in verse 38 "your cows" <note>original: "αἱ βόες σου"</note> (the manuscript has "your cows" <note>original: "αἱ βόαι σου"</note>) instead of "your goats" <note>original: "αἱ αἶγές σου"</note>; in verse 41 "for the sheep" <note>original: "τοῖς πρόβασιν"</note> (the rest "for the sheep" <note>original: "τοῖς προβάτοις"</note>); in verse 43 "all things that you see" <note>original: "ἃ σὺ ὁρᾶς ὅλα"</note> instead of "as many things as you see" <note>original: "ὅσα σὺ ὁρᾷς"</note>; in verse 44 "let us make a covenant" <note>original: "διαθήκην διαθησώμεθα"</note> (the rest "let me make" or "let us make a covenant" <note>original: "διαθῶμαι" or "διαθώμεθα διαθήκην"</note>); in the same place it omits "behold" <note>original: "ἴδε"</note> (as do two others, but followed by "but God"); in verse 47 "he called its name" instead of "he called it." These examples of readings prove sufficiently that the text of the most ancient copies has been, for the most part, faithfully preserved in our Bodleian manuscript: a fact best understood from the frequency of <term>Alexandrian forms</term> <note>Regional spelling variations in Greek common in manuscripts from Egypt</note>, which this codex maintains throughout even in places where they have long since been exchanged for common forms in most other copies. Pertaining to this—to list a few examples—are forms like "you shall take," "he shall take," "we shall take," "you were taken" <note>original: "λημψη, λημψεται, λημψομεθα, ελημφθης"</note> etc., which are usually though not everywhere used; "you have searched" <note>original: "ηραυνηκας"</note>, a form of the verb "to search" found here and there in the most ancient codices like the **Vatican**, **Alexandrian**, **Parisian Ephraem** <note>Codex Ephraemi Rescriptus, a famous 5th-century palimpsest</note> and a few similar ones; "sword" and "of a sword" <note>original: "μαχαιρη" and "μαχαιρης"</note>; "forty" <note>original: "τεσσερακοντα"</note> (often though not everywhere); "they found," "let it pass," "let it go before," "having become," "of old age," "yesterday" <note>original: "ευραν, παρελθατω, προελθατω, γεναμενοι, γηρους, εχθες"</note> (quite often; in Genesis 36:23 altered by a corrector to the more common spelling); "bring to me," "he was going up," "they were going up" <note>original: "ενενεγκον μοι, ανεβεννεν, ανεβεννον"</note> (very often, though the standard form is not avoided), "floodgates," "of the terebinth tree" <note>original: "καταρακται, τερεμινθου"</note>.

However, while praising this "Egyptian nobility," so to speak, of the codex, one must not forget that the scribe of the codex has given many proofs of either ignorance of the Greek language or negligence in writing—unless perhaps he found some of the faults we speak of already in the copy from which he transcribed. Examples of such faults (to which, it should be well noted, many other similarly troubled passages are added) are these: "sow seed" <note>original: "σπει σπερμα"</note> (Genesis 1:38)*; "he made" <note>original: "ἐποίη‾"</note> (3:15); "that [if] ever a day" <note>original: "οτι αν ημερα"</note> (the word "if" omitted, 3:27); "this only" <note>original: "τουτο μονου"</note> (4:3); "until returning" <note>original: "εως οὗ αποστρεψαι"</note> (4:8 and 9); "they were entering" <note>original: "εισπορευοντο"</note> (7:34); "of the four-sided" <note>original: "τετραγόνων"</note> (7:24); "you making" <note>original: "ποιείσης"</note> (7:33); "of foods" <note>original: "βροματων"</note> (8:18); "generation" <note>original: "γενᾶ" for "γενεᾶ"</note> (8:25); "two entered" <note>original: "δύο εισηλθεν"</note> (8:10); "he entered Noah" <note>original: "εισηλθεν νωε"</note> (the word "to" omitted, 8:31); "your sons" <note>original: "οι υῗ σου"</note> (9:37); "and have dominion" <note>original: "κὰ κατακυριεύσατε"</note> (10:30); "everlasting" <note>original: "ἀνíους" for "αἰωνíους"</note> (10:29); "Shem Japheth" <note>original: "σὴμ' ἴαφεθ"</note> (the word "and" omitted, 11:24); "let him dwell" <note>original: "κατοικησάτω"</note> (11:40); "them [neuter] by fire" <note>original: "αυτα" for "αυτας" [feminine]</note> (12:40); "instead of" <note>original: "ανθ"</note> (for "from" <note>original: "απο"</note>**) "the east" (16:20); "he pitched his tent."

*) 1:3. We published "in it" <note>original: "εν αυτω"</note>, but much more—the passage is very obscure—it seems to be written "into it" <note>original: "εισ αυτω"</note>: which ought to be amended to "into it" <note>original: "εις αυτο"</note> (though this is cited from no other codex) rather than "in it."

**) **ANΘ** <note>Instead of</note> for **AΠO** <note>From</note>: a substitution which undoubtedly flowed from a very ancient codex; for the oldest forms of these letters have a much greater similarity to one another than those which came into use later. <note>In ancient capital scripts, the Greek letters Nu (N) and Pi (Π) could look quite similar, leading to scribal errors.</note>

In this catalog, we have not included errors arising from **itacism** <note>Itacism is a common phenomenon in Greek manuscripts where different vowels (like 'i', 'ei', 'oi', 'u') began to be pronounced the same way, leading scribes to swap them accidentally.</note>; some of the more notable examples are these: **exelephthe** and **exelephthesan** (IX. A 15 and 19); **iste** (for "and unto") XVI. B 4; **you I will give** XXIX. B. 13; **these or the gates** XLVI. B 32; **hunter**, **he took away**, **he brought across**, **pillar**. (On the other hand, a printing error occurred in our own typeset edition at LVII. B 28: "of prosperity" for "of prosperity" <note>original: "ευθυνιας pro ευθηνιας"; the editor is noting a typo in his own work where he swapped an 'u' for an 'e'</note>.)

In the third place, we must speak of the **emendations** <note>emendations: corrections made to the text after it was first written</note> visible in the codex. Corrections were often made in this way—and indeed by the original scribe's hand or certainly a contemporary one—so that a letter completely omitted in the continuous text, or swapped for a similar one due to an itacism error, or even a syllable wrongly neglected in the text, was written above the line. Regarding these emendations, it is hardly worth examining them more closely: for all have been adopted into our printed text, nor is it important in most places to define with absolute certainty whether the original writer or another after him acted as the corrector. However, we will record in the index all places that have been touched by a correcting hand, marking those emendations that suggest a later period with a double asterisk.

III. B 1. he built <gloss>o</gloss>**
V. A 25. this <gloss>the</gloss>**

B 19 and following: **the chief cook of Pharaoh** — these words in lines 19 and 20 were written over very old erased text; however, the syllable **-ros** <note>original: "ρος"</note> at the start of line 21 was struck out by the same corrector with a small line drawn through it. LII. A 33. Joseph<sup>o</sup>** LII. B 12. the<sup>ei</sup> LIV. A 4. [unclear]<sup>o</sup>** LIV. B. 4. **all**: thus it was noted by the first hand itself. LVIII. A 37. it<sup>o</sup> (**?) LVIII. B 8. it shall be<sup>e</sup> (*?). These corrections should not be confused with those places where—as happens most often at the ends of lines—a letter is written above in the manner of an abbreviation, such as: was found<sup>o</sup>, of men<sup>s</sup>, from<sup>o</sup>, now<sup>o</sup>. Similarly, on page 222, XIX. B, in the last line, "days" is written below the line.

We must now move on to the system of accents and similar marks used in the Bodleian codex. It often reveals an unskillful scribe; yet in some places, it certainly deserves praise for consistency. Thus, prepositions often lack both accent and breathing marks or apostrophes, especially when combined with personal pronouns or followed by a vowel; for it is written: with me, upon me (but also 'upon me' with an apostrophe), concerning whom, through you, toward you, in the midst (this is found almost everywhere; conversely, it is usually written 'why' <note>original: "ινατί"</note> not 'why' <note>original: "ινατί" without the accent</note>), upon Abel, according to (but more often 'according to' with an accent) kind, according to the image (II. A 19. 'according to the image' with an apostrophe), according to the likeness, with you (but also 'with you' with an accent). The opposite rule applies to: to be called, the turning away, let them send away, also fifteen, governors. The rules regarding enclitics <note>Small words like "me," "you," or "is" that usually lose their accent to the preceding word.</note> are not entirely neglected. What use our codex makes of this will be clear from these examples: for I am, to multiply you, to be released from you, a bruise for me, which was (for 'was') to me, she is my sister, she is your wife, your brothers, where are they, these are, it is there, with you, for your sake, which (for 'which') is, which is. I have seen repeatedly

...he brought <note>original: "ἤγαγε"</note>. The imperative forms <note>A verb form used to give a command or request.</note> "say" <note>original: "εἶπον"</note> and "see" <note>original: "ἴδε"</note> are written as *say* <note>original: "εἶπον"</note> (XV. A 15.) and *see* <note>original: "ἴδε"</note> (XVI. A 33.). It is written as *and me* <note>original: "κἀμὲ"</note>, not *and me* <note>The author is noting a specific non-standard accentuation on the Greek word for "and me".</note>.

It is easy to bring forward many examples of the completely incorrect use of accents, unless perhaps one or two might be excused or explained by local dialects. Such examples include: *one voice* <note>original: "φωνῆ μία"</note>, *a spring was rising* <note>original: "πηγῆ ἀνέβαινεν"</note>, *all* <note>original: "πᾶσας"</note>, *tongues* <note>original: "γλῶσσας"</note>, *regions* <note>original: "χώραις"</note>, *of that woman* <note>original: "ἐκείνης"</note>, *of that man* <note>original: "ἐκείνου"</note>, *breads which* <note>original: "ἄρτους οὕς"</note>, *if* <note>original: "ἤν"</note> instead of *he/she/it was* <note>original: "ἦν"</note>, *the ungodly* <note>original: "ὁ ἀσεβῆς"</note>, *brothers* <note>original: "ἀδελφοῖ"</note>, *the one who pointed out* <note>original: "ὁ καταδείξας"</note>, *into synagogues* <note>original: "εἰς συναγωγᾶς"</note>, *to men* <note>original: "ἀνδρᾶσιν"</note>, *breadth* <note>original: "πλᾶτος"</note>, *having held back* <note>original: "ἐπισχῶν"</note>, *Keturah* <note>original: "χεττοῦρας"</note>, *the hunt* <note>original: "ἡ θῆρα"</note>, *the widow* <note>original: "ἡ χῆρα"</note>, *as if* <note>original: "ὡσεῖ"</note>, *reddish* <note>original: "πυρρακῆς"</note>, *to stand* <note>original: "ἑστᾶναι"</note> (and it is written as both *oak* <note>original: "δρῦν"</note> and *oak* <note>original: "δρὺν"</note>), *eight* <note>original: "ὀκτῶ"</note>, *we found* <note>original: "εὕρομεν"</note>, *we came* <note>original: "ἤλθομεν"</note>, *they pleased* <note>original: "ἤρεσαν"</note>, *he hoped* <note>original: "ἤλπισεν"</note>, *the son of Adas* <note>original: "ὁ υἱὸς ἀδὰς"</note>, *female* <note>original: "θήλυ"</note>, *these women* <note>original: "αὗται"</note>, *children* <note>original: "παῖδες"</note>, *Ishmaelites* <note>original: "ἰσμαηλῖται"</note>, *Canaanites* <note>original: "χαναναῖοι"</note>, *or* <note>original: "ἤ"</note> (instead of *the*) *day* <note>original: "ἡμέρᾳ"</note>, *cold* <note>original: "ψύχος"</note>, *may God enlarge* <note>original: "πλάτυναι ὁ θεός"</note>. Similarly, the rough and smooth breathings <note>Marks used in ancient Greek to indicate whether a initial vowel is preceded by an "h" sound (rough) or not (smooth).</note> are very often confused, just as we have frequently noted *he stood* <note>original: "ἔστησεν"</note>; likewise we have noted *well* <note>original: "εὖ"</note>, *you worked* <note>original: "εἰργάσω"</note>, *of the Egyptian* <note>original: "αἰγυπτίου"</note>, etc. The reflexive pronoun *of himself* <note>original: "αὑτοῦ"</note> is written almost as often as *of him* <note>original: "αὐτοῦ"</note> in very similar passages. The latter, however, is slightly more frequent. Thus, it is *of himself* <note>original: "αὑτοῦ"</note> in V. A 3. 16. 20. XI. A 19. XIV. A 19. 21. 22. 23. XVI. A. 10. and *of him* <note>original: "αὐτοῦ"</note> in II. B 12. IX. B 14 and (*to him*) 15. V. A 29 (*of herself* <note>original: "αὑτῆς"</note>).

Accents carry a unique importance in these cases: VII. B 28. *all the cattle* <note>original: "πάντὰ κτήνη"</note> (it should have been *all the* <note>original: "πάντα τὰ"</note>). A similar situation occurs at XVI. A 26. *he dwelt in Sodom* <note>original: "ἐσκηνωσέν σοδόμοις"</note>, which we touched upon above. LII. B 4. *I might leave behind* <note>original: "καταλειπῶ̄"</note>, in the same place 21. *having left behind* <note>original: "καταλειπὼν"</note>, XXXVII. A 30. *I will place here* <note>original: "θέσω δὲ"</note>, about which we have already spoken; for it should have been distinguished as *place here* <note>original: "θὲς ὧδε"</note>. Along with that, LI. B 22. *Israel saying* <note>original: "ἰσραὴλ λέγων"</note> (instead of *of the Ishmaelites* <note>original: "ἰσμαηλιτῶν"</note>) can be compared.

Since special attention must be paid to the systems of accents and breathings approved in ancient books, especially regarding proper names, I will list several whose spelling tends to vary in both published and handwritten books. It is written as: *Abraham* <note>original: "ἀβραὰμ"</note> (once *Abraham* <note>original: "ἄβρααμ"</note>), *Abram* <note>original: "ἄβραμ"</note>, *Hagar* <note>original: "ἀγγαὶ"</note>, *Haran* <note>original: "χάλεκ"</note>, *Oman* <note>original: "ὠμὰν"</note> and *-an* <note>original: "-ᾶν"</note>.

This also includes the apostrophe and diaeresis marks <note>Two dots placed over a vowel to show it is pronounced separately from the preceding vowel.</note>. We have represented the former everywhere in this edition where it was placed contrary to generally accepted grammatical usage, such as in: *Jacob’* <note>original: "ἰακωβ’"</note>, *Jared’* <note>original: "ἰαρεδ’"</note>, *Japheth’* <note>original: "ἰαφεθ’"</note>, *number* <note>original: "ἀριθ’μος"</note>, *had* <note>original: "ἐχ’"</note>, *Eden’* <note>original: "εδεμ’"</note>, *I will pour out’* <note>original: "γχμ’"</note>, *in the middle* <note>original: "εμ’μεσω"</note>, *coal’* <note>original: "ανθραξ’"</note>, *water’* <note>original: "υδωρ’"</note>, *Lot’* <note>original: "λωτ’"</note>, *Lamech’* <note>original: "λαμεχ’"</note>. We have taken care to reproduce the diaeresis marks in proper names according to the reliability of the codex <note>A "codex" is an ancient manuscript in book form rather than a scroll.</note>. Even in these, they are sometimes placed and sometimes not; the usage fluctuates most in those cases where a breathing mark is added, just as *Jacob* <note>original: "ἰακὼβ"</note>, *Jacob* <note>original: "ἰακωβ̈"</note>, *Jacob* <note>original: "ἰ̈ακὼβ"</note> is placed; likewise *Judah* <note>original: "ἰοῦδας"</note>, *Judah* <note>original: "ἰ̈οῦδας"</note>, *Judah* <note>original: "ἰ̈οῦδᾱς"</note>. Furthermore, words of this kind such as *early* <note>original: "πρωΐ"</note>, *son* <note>original: "υἱὸς"</note>, *sons* <note>original: "υἱοὶ"</note>, *garment* <note>original: "ἱ̈μάτιον"</note>, *carried up* <note>original: "ἀνενηχτ̈α"</note> (thus with the 'no' <note>original: "νο"</note> omitted), *the office of chief cupbearer* <note>original: "ἀρχιοινοχοΐα"</note> often have the same dots, except that in these too the breathing mark itself acts to signify the diaeresis. (Hence it is not *sons* <note>original: "υἱοὶ"</note> but *sons* <note>original: "υἱ̈οὶ"</note>.)

The punctuation <note>The Latin term used here is "interpunctio".</note> is quite varied. For besides the high, middle, and low points, double points are sometimes used, as in XIX. A 15. XXXVI. B 34.; likewise the comma is frequent; here and there the caret sign (^) is found, the meaning of which roughly corresponds to the comma; likewise the question mark (;) comes into use, though the comma holds its place here and there (as in V. A 13 and 22.). However, although the number of punctuation marks is so great, it also happens that where punctuation is absolutely required—and even a major one—it is completely omitted; cf. V. B 15. between *Gaidad’* <note>original: "γαϊδαδ’"</note> and *and* <note>original: "και"</note>, XLII. B 24. between *Jacob’* <note>original: "ἰακωβ’"</note> and *he* <note>original: "αυτος"</note>, XLV. A in the second to last verse between *Esau* <note>original: "ησαυ"</note> and *he* <note>original: "αυτος"</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of scribal conventions, specifically abbreviations (compendia), before beginning a new section (IV) describing two extremely old palimpsest fragments of the Gospel of John found in the British Museum's Nitrian collection.</meta>

<page-num>XXXXII</page-num>

The use of scribal abbreviations <term>compendia|shortened forms of words used by scribes to save space</term> is extremely frequent. We have neglected almost none of them in this edition. Almost none, I say; for the manner in which alpha <note>original: "α"</note> and upsilon <note>original: "υ"</note> and similar letters are joined more closely—see in our specimen the word *themselves* <note>original: "αuτοι"</note> in line 3—we have not expressed by imitation. In addition to the most common abbreviations for *Lord* <note>original: "KC̅"</note>, *God* <note>original: "ΘC̅"</note>, *Heaven* <note>original: "OYNOC̅"</note>, *Man* <note>original: "ANOC̅"</note>, etc.,*) there is also the word *Virgin* <note>original: "Π̇AΡNOC̅", an abbreviation for "Parthenos"</note>; likewise, the letters *ou* <note>original: "oυ"</note> and *pr* <note>original: "πρ"</note> are often contracted, for which we have accounted by placing the *u* <note>original: "υ"</note> and *r* <note>original: "ρ"</note> in a higher position. Elsewhere in the manuscript itself, just as is done in the edition, a letter is written above the line. Somewhat more unusual is the method by which the word *the* <note>original: "την", the feminine accusative article</note> is expressed: compare IX. B 23. XIV. B 21. In the manuscript, it is not so much like a J~ as it is a T with a superscript A <note>original: "T<sup>A</sup>"</note>. Truly, the most common abbreviation for the writer is the word *and* <note>original: "και"</note>, which we have rendered with a unique sign. You can see the form written in the manuscript itself in the engraved stone specimen.

->### IV.<-

->## TWO PALIMPSEST FRAGMENTS OF THE GOSPEL OF JOHN.<-

British Museum Manuscript marked with the number 17,136—which is among those recently brought to London from the Nitrian Desert <note>A region in Egypt famous for its ancient monasteries where many early manuscripts were preserved.</note>—contains the hymns of Severus in Syriac in a sextodecimo <term>sextodecimo|a small book size roughly 4 by 6 inches</term> format. Four leaves of this book, marked with numbers 117 and 118, 127 and 128, were rewritten <term>palimpsest|a manuscript page from which the original text has been scraped or washed off so that it can be used again</term> after some Greek biblical text had been erased. When the curator of Syriac books, a man by far most skilled in Oriental literature <note>This likely refers to the scholar William Cureton, who managed the British Museum's Syriac collections.</note>, pointed this out to me, I applied myself with all zeal to reading the Greek. Having inspected these more carefully, I understood that a double Syriac script had been superimposed, from which it is obvious how much the difficulty of reading is increased. It happened, however, that with the exception of only one place where the reading remained doubtful to me, I read and transcribed whatever remained of the Greek letters. Although these remains fill four leaves of the Syriac book as it now exists, because of its very small size they comprise scarcely more than ten verses of the sacred text, specifically from the Gospel of John. These are, however, the remains of a manuscript whose age few others equal, and even fewer surpass. The style of the uncial <term>uncial|a script written entirely in large, rounded capital letters used from the 4th to 8th centuries</term> letters is pure, and the punctuation is very simple, although in that regard it yields to the Codex Friderico-Augustanus and the Vatican Bible <note>The Codex Vaticanus, one of the oldest and most important biblical manuscripts.</note>, in which it is well known that punctuation is extremely rare. Indeed, it stands out among most Greek manuscripts that we are accustomed to assigning to the highest antiquity because, like a few others, it lacks the use of what they call initial letters <note>Large decorated or enlarged letters at the beginning of a section or paragraph.</note>. Hence, for one defining its age, it will hardly be possible to doubt whether it was written in the fifth or the fourth century. We are not prevented from preferring the fourth century by the Ammonian numbers <term>Ammonian numbers|a system of numbering the sections of the Gospels used for cross-referencing, often attributed to Ammonius of Alexandria</term> added to the text; for although there is not yet any trace of these in the Vatican book (and in that one alone), nevertheless Epiphanius and Jerome are witnesses that they were already in use and authority in the fourth century. Furthermore, the text itself agrees in several places with those witnesses who are agreed to hand down the most ancient version of the text—

---

*) XL. B. 15. We have published *opo* <note>original: "oπo"</note> (instead of *opo* with a line <note>original: "oπo̅"</note>) according to the reliability of the manuscript; for in the manuscript itself, the horizontal line <note>A line placed over letters to indicate a contraction or abbreviation.</note> was omitted.

<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of the Gospel of John fragments, focusing on specific textual variants and the physical reconstruction of the manuscript's original layout before introducing new fragments of Ezekiel and Kings.</meta>

...is simultaneous. Thus, in John 13:19, the reading "believe when it happens" <note>original Greek: "πιστ. οταν γενηται"</note> is supported—along with the manuscripts B, L, and Origen—by these Greek sources alone; likewise in verse 24, "tell who it is" <note>original Greek: "ειπε τις εστιν"</note> is supported with those same manuscripts and Codex C, X, and 33; and similarly in verse 26, "of Iscariot" <note>original Greek: "ισκαριωτου"</note>. It occurs slightly differently in 16:12 and 13, where it certainly does not align with B and L. Conversely, in verse 16, it again confirms the reading of codices B, D, and L. Regarding verse 19, where it is not entirely clear which reading it holds, see below.

The writing that survives on these eight pages, as already indicated and published by us in a style mimicking the original handwriting, makes up half-portions of the eight columns as they once existed in the complete <term>codex|an ancient book made of stacked sheets (leaves) rather than a scroll</term>. Consequently, between individual fragments, approximately as many verses are missing as those that remain; for it seems a full column comprised 16 or 18 lines. On the margins of four columns (311. A and D, 312. A and D), some of the <term>parchment|animal skin prepared for writing</term> has been cut away, along with the final part of several lines. Regarding the small line we expressed in the stone-engraved plate under 7<sup>b</sup>, placed over the first letter of the word "upon" <note>original Greek: "επι"</note> (page 312. C), it does not belong to the <term>breathing marks|signs used in Greek to indicate aspiration</term>, but is rather a sign by which the beginnings of columns seem to have been marked in the codex. We observed the same sign over the 'o' in "when" <note>original Greek: "οταν"</note> (p. 311. C), although in this location it seemed more like an <term>asper|a "rough" breathing mark indicating an 'h' sound</term>.

In the portions we transcribed from page 311, the letters written under Column C, line 5, after "if" <note>original: "αν"</note>—namely "whom I shall send" <note>original Greek: "τινα πεμψω"</note>—are slightly more hidden than the rest; however, they are hardly in doubt. On page 312, under Column C, line 5, I regret that I have not sufficiently grasped the script of the codex. I seemed to see "Jesus knew" <note>original Greek: "εγνω ισ" with an abbreviation bar over "ισ" for "Iesus"</note>, so that to complete the verse nothing would need to be added except "that the" <note>original Greek: "οτι η"</note>, for "wishing" <note>original Greek: "θελων"</note> in line 6 stands by probability. If this reading is true, this new witness again confirms codices D, L, and B. But it was rendered most doubtful to me because it must be conjectured that more than ten letters are contained in this line, although it is easily perceived that there is great inequality in that regard. For on the same page under Column A, line 2, only eight letters are held; in the third, nine; in the fifth, ten; in the fourth, thirteen; and in the seventh, twelve. Having compared these, I cannot help but consider the proposed reading especially probable.

Furthermore, I take it upon myself here that, regarding the passages of the codices published in this volume whose reading has not had all doubt removed for me, as soon as it happens that I repeat my English journey, they will be treated with new studies by me. If I obtain any new light from this, it shall be added with all care to the volumes of the *Sacred Monuments* <note>original Latin: "Monumentorum sacrorum"</note> that follow.

## V. AND VI.

# TWO PALIMPSEST FRAGMENTS, THE FIRST (V.) OF THE PROPHET EZEKIEL, THE SECOND (VI.) OF THE THIRD BOOK OF KINGS.

Among the same Syriac codices of the British Museum recently brought to England from the <term>Nitrian|The Nitrian Desert in Egypt, famous for its early Christian monasteries</term> monastery of the Copts, these two scattered <term>palimpsest|a manuscript page from which the original text has been scraped off so the surface can be reused</term> leaves were found. One contains a fragment of the prophet Ezekiel, the other of the Third Book of Kings <note>Known as 1 Kings in modern English Bibles</note>. The former, whose text was rendered clearer by the application of chemicals, contains parts of Ezekiel...

<meta>This page transitions from the physical description of the Gospel of John fragments to an analysis of Old Testament fragments, specifically from Ezekiel and the Book of Kings (referred to here as 3 Kings, known in modern Bibles as 1 Kings). ...we have recognized this to be easy. It possesses a handwriting similar to the <term>palimpsest|a manuscript page, either from a scroll or a book, from which the text has been scraped or washed off so that it can be used again</term> fragments of the <term>Octateuch|the first eight books of the Old Testament</term>—specifically those in the Tischendorf II collection of the Leipzig University Library, which have an Arabic text written over them. These were transcribed by us in the first volume of the *Sacred Monuments* <note>original Latin: "Monumentorum sacrorum"</note>, under number III, and represented with an accurate specimen (Table III, no. III). Just like those, it is appropriate to assign this one to the seventh century AD. The style of the writing, just as in those others, reveals an Eastern scribe rather than a Greek one. Furthermore, both seem to share common grammatical forms: for instance, the form "I shall take" <note>original Greek: "λημψομαι"</note> is used in this fragment with great consistency (five times). The punctuation in two places, as we have published it, occupies a middle rather than a high position. The <term>apostrophe|a mark used in ancient manuscripts to indicate a pause or to separate words</term> is placed twice, just as it is accustomed to be placed in the most ancient <term>codices|ancient books made of bound leaves</term>. Following the loyalty to the manuscript, we have expressed the <term>compendium|a specialized abbreviation</term> for the syllable "ou" <note>original Greek: "ου"</note> on page 314, column 1, line 8; likewise, the so-called <term>diaeresis|two dots placed over a vowel to show it is pronounced separately</term> points, and the oblong lines by which the first two letters of the word "son" <note>original Greek: "υιε" (elsewhere "ϋϊε")</note> are marked in the manner of diaeresis points. Whatever we have included in brackets or indicated only with dots on page 313, column 2 and page 314, column 1, all of those perished when the margin was cut away. On page 314, column 1, line 10, we have published the letters "...otar" <note>original Greek: "οταρ"</note>: from this, given the space on line 9 for "and" <note>original Greek: "κα"</note> and line 11 for "the" <note>original Greek: "τον"</note>, it must be concluded that the letters for "and t" <note>original Greek: "και τ"</note> have perished in this same line.

This fragment of Ezekiel (4:16–5:4) is even surpassed in its claim to antiquity by another fragment plucked from the books of Kings: 3 Kings 8:58–9:1 <note>Note: In many older traditions, 1 Kings is titled 3 Kings</note>. The text of this, which we have published, is covered by a double layer of later writing: one is Syriac, and the other—which predates the Syriac in age—is Coptic or, as it seems more likely, Greek <term>minuscule|a style of lowercase writing</term>. The <term>uncial|a style of large, rounded capital letters</term> letters in which it is written have such a great similarity to Coptic script that, before I had seen and read it, they were considered to be Coptic. We treated a similar handwriting, though already deviating in some forms from the most ancient style, in the first volume of the *Sacred Monuments*, the fourth section of which (page XXXIV and following) should be consulted in the Prolegomena. Indeed, the writing of this fragment approaches a greater similarity to the Borgian fragments of the Gospel of John, which the monk George once published in Rome and which we ourselves have recently and accurately depicted. For that reason, I do not doubt that this fragment from the British Museum, just like the Borgian ones, should be referred to the fifth century. It also has this in common with the Borgian fragments—as well as with those published in our first volume—that, as our table provides for viewing, the initial letters differ only slightly from the size of the rest. Most of the punctuation marks occupy a middle rather than a high position; care was taken so that we might express this accurately in our edition. However, the fact that it makes no difference whether a point is placed in the middle or at the top is made clear by the fact that even at the end of a major section, a middle point is found here and there.

On page 315, column 1, at the end of line 10, where a point had undoubtedly been placed by the writer, the <term>membrane|the animal skin or parchment used for writing</term> is damaged. Hence, we have added a point enclosed in brackets. Similarly, the letters we have included in brackets throughout—especially on page 315, the first column, and page 316, the second column—were all removed when the margins were cut off. Finally, the things we have published as errors on page 315, second column, lines 19 and 22, namely "was" <note>original Greek: "ην"</note> and "thousands" <note>original Greek: "χιλιαδες"</note>, originated from the writer of the codex himself.

<summary>This page provides a technical paleographic analysis of two biblical fragments—Ezekiel and 1 Kings—dating them to the 7th and 5th centuries respectively based on handwriting style and punctuation. The author highlights the physical damage to the parchment margins and compares the scripts to other known manuscripts like the Borgian fragments.</summary>
<keywords>paleography, Ezekiel, Book of Kings, Tischendorf, uncial, palimpsest, Coptic script, manuscript dating, codicology, British Museum</keywords>
