<meta>This page transitions from the physical analysis of New Testament fragments and historical Old Testament books (Ezekiel and Kings) to the description of a newly discovered, highly ancient fragmentary Psalter found during the author's second journey to the East.</meta>

# VII.

## FRAGMENT OF THE PSALMS.

I discovered these remarkable remains of a very ancient <term>Psalter|a volume containing the Book of Psalms</term> during my second journey to the East. They had been glued to the cover of a more recent handwritten book. Because of this foreign use to which they had been subjected for a long time, both the parchment and the text suffered no small amount of damage. Since the small sheets of which they consist are torn almost through the middle and are mutilated in many places, quite a few letters have perished entirely; others, especially those written on the more delicate parts of the parchment, do not appear unless viewed against the light. However, we spared no effort to read and publish whatever letters remained, in whatever condition they were found.

The parchment is not of excessive thinness. The individual verses are guided by lines drawn with a <term>stylus|a needle-like tool used to score lines into parchment</term>, and these are not double lines, as is done commonly elsewhere, but single ones, positioned so that the letters sit upon the lines. The ink, where it is sufficiently preserved, is not yellowish but brown in color. The title of Psalm 142 was written in <term>minium|a reddish-orange lead pigment often used for headings</term>, and if I am not mistaken, it was not written with a reed pen but painted with a brush; this is why most of the letters—as accurately shown in our plate—have a form slightly different from the continuous text and similar manuscripts. There is no use of so-called initial letters <note>large capital letters at the start of sections</note> in this fragment: this is confirmed by the beginning of Psalm 142, where the text starts with an ordinary letter just like the title. The style of the <term>uncial|a script written entirely in large, rounded capital letters</term> letters reflects the highest antiquity, very similar to the Vatican fragments of <term>Cassius Dio|a Roman historian whose 3rd-century works survive in very old fragments</term>. This being the case, I do not doubt that this Psalter—of which we have nothing but this small but precious part—was written at least in the fifth century. The system of division used in the text, expressed by letters of the alphabet, is not known to me from any other source. Clear traces of it remain in Psalm 144; on the other page, the numbers that had been placed there perished when the margin was cut away. There is no need to speak further on the <term>stichometric|writing where each line corresponds to a specific unit of measure or poetic verse</term> arrangement: for the ancient writers themselves, such as Gregory of Nazianzus, testify that the Psalter, along with several other books, was customarily written in verses as early as the first centuries after Christ.

On page 319, we have transcribed the more delicate part of the parchment, in which most things have somewhat faded, except for the ninth and tenth verses containing the title written in red ink, which are better preserved than the rest. In verse 10, we have rendered the dots over the "u" <note>original Greek: "υ" (upsilon)</note> because they are very clear. However, I do not know whether similar dots were originally placed over the "i" <note>original Greek: "ι" (iota)</note> that follows, but were later lost to a tear. In verse 14, "righteousness" <note>original Greek: "δικαιωσυνη"</note> clearly appears. Furthermore, it has already been noted that the outer margin—where it is probable that numbers were added in the same manner as on the other page—has been cut off. Page 320, which displays the sturdier part of the parchment, allows many things to be exceptionally well-recognized. Some parts, however, were lost not only to torn areas but also because the first and second verses are partially covered by leather. The number attached to the third verse is certain. The one that follows seems to have been present but perished: for I certainly seemed to see the small line placed over the number; nor is it credible that the number 38 <note>original Greek: "$\overline{\lambda\eta}$"</note> should be followed by the number 19 <note>original Greek: "$\overline{\iota\theta}$"</note>. Regarding the form "Labed" <note>original Greek: "$\lambda\alpha\beta\epsilon\delta$" — a Greek transliteration of the Hebrew letter Lamed</note> written in verse 15, compare "Labd" <note>original Greek: "$\lambda\alpha\beta\delta$"</note>, which the <term>Codex Alexandrinus|a 5th-century manuscript of the Greek Bible</term> and the <term>Codex Frederico-Augustanus|parts of the 4th-century Codex Sinaiticus discovered by Tischendorf</term> provide in the Lamentations of Jeremiah.

<summary>This page describes the discovery and physical characteristics of a 5th-century Greek Psalter fragment found recycled in a later book binding. The author analyzes its unique paleography, red-ink titles, and ancient verse-by-verse formatting.</summary>
<keywords>Psalter, manuscript recovery, uncial script, stichometry, minium ink, paleography, Codex Alexandrinus, Psalm 142, Psalm 144</keywords>

# VIII.

## FRAGMENT OF THE BOOK OF GENESIS.

These fragments of the twenty-fourth chapter of the Book of Genesis, printed above on page 321, are contained on a small scrap of parchment that I found among discarded leaves in the middle of a volume during that same eastern journey I recently mentioned. There can be no doubt that they formed part of the same manuscript from which I obtained leaves in 1844, which I named the <term>Codex Friderico-Augustanus|A famous 4th-century Greek manuscript of the Bible, named in honor of the King of Saxony; it is now known to be part of the Codex Sinaiticus</term> in honor of the late King of Saxony. It recently became necessary to repeat and confirm our opinion that this manuscript was written in the fourth century after Christ, when we were inserting another fragment of the same codex—taken from the books of Isaiah and Jeremiah and previously transcribed by us in the East—into the first volume of this *Collection of Sacred Monuments*. Our Genesis fragment formed the lowest part of a leaf; although it has space for more than twenty lines, it contains only eleven written lines. The parchment is extremely thin. On the front page, the writing is excellently preserved; however, on the other side, due to the thinness of the parchment, no letter has fully retained its color.

Traces of a corrector are visible here and there, which we have not represented in the published columns. For he placed dots <note>indicative of punctuation or pauses</note> after <note>original Greek: "καμήλους" (camels)</note> in verse 3, after <note>original Greek: "αυτου" (his)</note> in verse 9, and after <note>original Greek: "μεσοποταμιαν" (Mesopotamia)</note> in verse 11; likewise, in verse 8, he restored the letters <note>original Greek: "το" (the)</note>. And these marks indeed refer to the firmer side of the parchment. What the corrector may have provided on the other page is less clear; however, he seems to have punctuated after <note>original Greek: "μου" (my)</note> and after <note>original Greek: "νυν" (now)</note> in verse 7, and likewise after <note>original Greek: "υδατος" (water)</note> in verse 11. Conversely, the marks we have expressed after <note>original Greek: "πορευομαι" (I go)</note> and <note>original Greek: "επ αυτην" (upon it)</note> are likely to be attributed to the original scribe of the manuscript. The parts we have occasionally enclosed in brackets have been cut off. Among these, in column 1, verse 8, the letter <note>original Greek: "ε" (epsilon)</note> seemed no more doubtful than the <note>original Greek: "σ" (sigma)</note> in verse 9.

# IX.

## TWO FRAGMENTS OF THE GOSPEL OF MATTHEW.

The fragments of the twelfth chapter of the Gospel of Matthew, which are read on the lower part of page 321, are contained on two small scraps recently discovered by us in an Arabic <term>codex|an ancient manuscript in book form</term>, where they had been used in the binding. They had formerly belonged to the same manuscript of which some remains were brought by us from the East in 1844 and placed in the Leipzig University Library under the name <term>Codex Tischendorfianus I|A collection of Greek manuscript fragments named after the author, Constantin von Tischendorf</term>, and shortly thereafter published in our earlier *Collection of Unpublished Sacred Monuments*, with an added specimen of several lines <term>engraved on stone|a reference to lithographic printing used to replicate ancient handwriting</term>. The punctuation we have displayed, as well as the <term>breathing mark|A small mark used in Greek to indicate the presence or absence of an 'h' sound at the start of a word</term> in line 2, were added by the original scribe of the manuscript himself. 

As for the <term>accents|Marks used in Greek to indicate pitch or stress</term> which a later <term>corrector|A person, often centuries later, who edited or updated the text of a manuscript</term> placed on most of the words, we have found it sufficient to reproduce those in the <term>facsimile|A precise reproduction or copy of the original script and layout</term>. Additionally, in line 8, he placed these marks: "this is" <note>original Greek: οὗτος ἐστὶν</note>.

And they understood none of these things; and this saying was hidden from them.

...of them does she become the wife? For the seven had her as a wife. And answering, he said to them: "The sons of this age <note>original: "αἰῶνος" (aionos); refers to the current world system or era</note> marry and are given in marriage. But those considered worthy to attain to that age and the resurrection from the dead neither marry nor are given in marriage. For they can no longer die, for they are like angels <column-break/> and are sons of God, being sons of the resurrection. But that the dead are raised, even Moses revealed in the passage concerning the bush <note>The "Burning Bush" episode in Exodus 3:6</note>, when he calls the Lord the God of Abraham and the God of Isaac and the God of Jacob. Now He is not God of the dead, but of the living; for all live to Him." Then some of the scribes <note>term: "γραμματέων" (grammateon); scholars or experts in the Jewish Law</note> answering said, "Teacher, you have spoken well."

# LVIII. B.

And Laban said to Jacob:
"Why did you flee in secret and deceive me? And you led away my daughters like captives of the sword. You did not tell me, nor did you send me away with joy and with music, with tambourines and the lyre. You did not allow me to kiss my children and my daughters; now you have acted foolishly. And now my hand has the power to do you harm; but the God of your father spoke to me yesterday, saying to me: 'Watch yourself, lest you speak to Jacob anything harsh.' Now therefore you have gone, for you longed greatly to depart to the house of your father; but why did you steal my gods?" <note>original: "θεος" (theos) - Laban refers here to his household idols, the teraphim.</note>

And Jacob answered and said to Laban: "I said, 'Lest you should take your daughters from me by force.' But with whomever you find your gods, he shall not live. In the presence of our kinsmen, identify what is yours with me and take it."

But Jacob did not know that Rachel his wife had stolen them.

And Laban went in and searched...

<meta>LXVI. B. GENESIS 35, 5–14. This portion of the manuscript uses "Nomina Sacra," which are shortened forms of sacred names like God (ΘC) and Israel (ΙΗΛ), traditionally marked with a horizontal bar in Greek manuscripts.</meta>

<summary>This page describes Jacob’s departure from Shechem under divine protection and his arrival at Luz, where God reaffirms the covenant made with his ancestors. The text highlights the formal renaming of Jacob to Israel and the promise that he will father a multitude of nations and kings.</summary>

--- Page 244 ---
<meta>This page represents the opening of the Book of Genesis from a 5th-century style Greek manuscript. The text is written in uncial script (all capital letters) using scriptio continua—a style where words are run together without spaces. The translation below introduces spaces for readability while maintaining the original line-by-line structure.</meta>

# GENESIS

<summary>This page records the completion of the third day of creation—the appearance of dry land and vegetation—and the beginning of the fourth day, where God creates the sun, moon, and stars to govern time and provide light.</summary>

<summary>This page records the climax of the Creation week, including the creation of humanity in the image of God, the divine command to be fruitful, and the establishment of the seventh day as a hallowed day of rest.</summary>
